<meta>This page serves as the frontispiece for the manuscript, featuring a detailed allegorical engraving that sets the thematic tone for the work.</meta>

-> # **Frontispiece** <-

<note>The page consists of a full-page allegorical engraving intended to represent the themes of time, divine will, and the cosmos.</note>

-> Engraved by: Ukhtomsky <note>original: "Грав: Ухтомской." This refers to Andrey Grigoryevich Ukhtomsky (1771–1852), a renowned copper engraver and member of the Russian Imperial Academy of Arts.</note> <-

**Visual Description:**
The composition is dominated by the figure of **Father Time** <term>Chronos; the personification of time</term>, depicted as a powerful, bearded man with large wings. He rests his head upon his hand in a melancholic or thoughtful pose, holding his traditional **scythe**—a symbol of the harvesting of lives and the inevitable passage of years. Beside him sits a large **celestial globe** or sphere, intricately marked with spiral lines representing the movements of the heavens.

Above this earthly and temporal scene, an **angel** flies through the clouds, sounding a long trumpet to announce a revelation or the fame of the subject matter. In the highest corner, the **Eye of Providence** <note>represented by a radiant triangle within a sun</note> emits rays of light that pierce through the dark, dramatic clouds, signifying that all human time and celestial motion occur under the watchful eye of the Divine.

<summary>A formal frontispiece featuring an allegorical engraving by A. Ukhtomsky, depicting Father Time, a celestial sphere, and the Eye of Providence. It serves to introduce the manuscript's themes of eternity, divine oversight, and the cosmic order.</summary>

<keywords>Father Time, Chronos, scythe, celestial globe, Providence, Andrey Ukhtomsky, allegory, engraving, Russian art</keywords>

# THE KEY

->TO THE<-

## MYSTERIES OF NATURE,

->a work by<-

## *MR. ECKARTSHAUSEN.*

---

### *PART I.*

<image-desc>An allegorical engraving featuring a central figure of a bearded man with feathered wings on his head <note>The wings on the head often identify the figure as Hermes or Mercury, the traditional messenger of the gods and the patron of "hermetic" or hidden wisdom.</note>, his torso emerging from the body of a classical lyre. He extends his arms to the sides, holding a small globe in his right hand and a radiant sun in his left, both connected to the lyre by heavy chains <note>This visual metaphor suggests that the sun and the planets are bound together by a single, divine "harmony" or law, much like the strings of a musical instrument.</note>. The lyre rests atop a stack of five large books from which several seals or medals on ribbons hang. This assembly sits on a stone plinth draped with a fringed cloth adorned with astrological symbols. On the face of the plinth is the inscription "THE LYRE OF THE WORLD-HARMONY" <note>original Latin: "LIRA ZARABTALAM." The term "Zarabtalam" is a mystical cipher used by Eckartshausen to refer to the secret resonance of the universe.</note>. Sparse vegetation grows at the base of the monument.</image-desc>

on the condition that after printing, and before being released from the printing house, seven copies of this book shall be submitted to the Censorship Committee for delivery to the appropriate authorities in accordance with the laws. <note>In 19th-century Russia, publishers were required to provide "legal deposit" copies to the state for record-keeping and further monitoring.</note> Moscow, May 24th, 1820.

## To the Reader.

We are fulfilling the promise we made to continue publishing the works of this beloved Author. It would have been proper for this book to appear before you first, kind Reader, since it was the first by which we came to know the Author and because it was written before those which we have already published: for its first volume was printed in a second edition <note>original: "тисненіемъ" — literally "pressing," an archaic term for an edition or printing</note> as early as 1791, while the fourth appeared in the year 1792. However, to undertake from the start the publication of a book consisting of several parts before the Author had become known to the public would have been risky; moreover, other circumstances did not permit it.

The Author <note>original: "Сочинитель" — composer or writer</note> states that his intention in writing this book was to make people more attentive to Nature <note>original: "Натурѣ" — Nature, here referring to the divinely ordered natural world</note>, and to prove as much as possible that one should not believe everything, yet nor should one reject everything; showing partly through theoretical observations and partly through practical experiments that much which is miraculous and incredible is worthy of impartial <note>The text ends mid-sentence as the page concludes.</note>

<summary>In this preface, the publishers explain that although this book was written before the author's other works, it is only being released now that his reputation is established. The author's stated goal is to encourage a balanced study of nature, avoiding both blind faith and total skepticism regarding the miraculous.</summary>
<keywords>preface, publishing history, nature, skepticism, miracles, scientific method, 18th-century literature</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>Preface page IV; continuation from page III. This page completes the opening sentence regarding "impartial investigation" and provides instructions to the reader on how to approach the text.</meta>

investigation and impartial research, asserting moreover that truth and wisdom should be sought only in God, that the path to them consists in drawing nearer to the Divinity, and that the more we uncover the causes of the existence of things in <term>Nature</term> <note>The author uses the term "Натура" (Natura) to refer to the physical world as a divinely ordered system that reveals the Creator's wisdom.</note>, the more the Divinity will appear to us worthy of worship, and His laws more sacred and beneficial.

He asks—as do we of our readers—not to rush to a conclusion about the book based on any single chapter without considering its connection to those preceding and following it; and not merely to adopt concepts from the Author’s own ideas, but to compare them with the reality itself and seek the truth with a pure heart. Should anything incomprehensible be encountered, it is likely because the Reader, perhaps, does not yet possess the preliminary knowledge required to grasp higher forms of understanding. In such a case, let him set the book aside and strive first to acquire that foundational knowledge mentioned within this very book; only then should he return to analyze the difficult passages. Generally speaking, this book was not written for <term>drones</term> <note>original: "трутни" (trutni). A metaphor for the intellectually idle or those who expect to gain wisdom without personal effort or study.</note> who

<summary>The author argues that studying the laws of Nature leads to a deeper worship of God and cautions readers to study the text methodically, acquiring necessary foundational knowledge before judging difficult sections.</summary>
<keywords>divinity, Nature, seeking truth, spiritual study, intellectual discipline, preliminary knowledge</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>Preface page V; continuation from page IV. This page concludes the translator's advice to the reader and introduces the author's original dedication to his patron, Elector Karl Theodor of Bavaria.</meta>

not only refuse to take the trouble to reflect and investigate, nor even wish to think in the slightest; nor is it for those clever wits who know and understand everything at a single glance: but rather for sincere seekers of truth, who allow themselves to be led so that they may later walk on their own.

The Author, in dedicating this work to the late <term>Elector</term> <note>original: "Курфирстъ" (Kurfürst). A high-ranking prince of the Holy Roman Empire who held the right to participate in the election of the Emperor. The author, Karl von Eckartshausen, served at the court of Karl Theodor in Munich.</note> of Bavaria, Karl Theodor, in whose service he was, says in his dedication:

> "I sincerely confess to Your Electoral Serenity, that as much
> "as I love humanity in general, and as much as
> "I desire to serve the lowliest of my
> "fellow-citizens: so much do I fear
> "the company of the many; for my way
> "of thinking agrees with but very few;
> "and I truly feel that the more
> "often I am among men, the
> "less I return to myself as a 
> "man."

In this instance, the translator is in surprising agreement with the Author; and perhaps this similarity of sentiment is the cause of his labors, through which he desires to serve you, kind and well-intentioned Reader!

The illustration included with this book is self-explanatory. The vignette, however—

<summary>The translator concludes his opening remarks by distinguishing between lazy readers and sincere seekers, then quotes the author’s dedication to the Elector of Bavaria regarding the spiritual exhaustion of social life.</summary>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the translator's preface, moving from the author's dedication to a detailed symbolic explanation of the engraving found on the book's title page. The previous page ended by introducing the vignette.</meta>

<page-num>VI</page-num>

on the title page, which we have retained from the Author, presents a symbolic image found in the year 1556 in Ethiopia, with the inscription: *Lika Zarabshalam*: which means **Creator of Worlds**. The Author explains this figure in the following way.

The **Head of the Old Man** represents the Creator of worlds, who created all things, the <term>Unity</term> <note>In mystical philosophy, the "Unity" or "Monad" refers to the single, indivisible source from which all existence originates.</note>. The **three flames** surrounding his head are a Symbol of perfection: in the physical world they signify length, breadth, and depth; in the spiritual, thought, mind, and soul; in relation to bodies, number, measure, and weight; in relation to the soul, reason, memory, and will. **Two hands**, emerging from a single head, represent the first number that issued from the unity—the number of creation—a symbol of production. In the hands, the **Sun and the Earth** are bound by a chain. The **body with outstretched arms** is a sign of accord, or Harmony. The **Books** upon which it rests are the seven books of the mysteries of <term>Nature</term> <note>Original: "Натура" (Natura). In this context, it refers to the divinely ordered natural world and its hidden laws.</note>, sealed with seven seals. The **four strings** on the instrument are the sign of the <term>tetrachord</term> <note>A four-stringed scale used in ancient Greek music theory; here it represents the mathematical foundation of cosmic harmony.</note> (four-stringed arrangement), by which the first musical concert was invented: it is also a symbol of regularity; for they signify the point, the line,

<summary>This page provides a detailed allegorical interpretation of the title page's engraving, an Ethiopian symbol representing the "Creator of Worlds" and the mathematical/spiritual laws of the universe.</summary>
<keywords>symbolism, Ethiopia, Creator of Worlds, Unity, Trinity, Harmony, seven seals, tetrachord, mysticism</keywords>

*surface and thickness. By this **hieroglyph*** <note>original: "іероглифомъ" — in the 18th century, "hieroglyph" was often used to describe any symbolic or mystical image intended to be decoded for deeper meaning.</note> *is expressed all **Nature*** <term>Nature</term> <note>original: "Натура" (Natura). In this philosophical context, it refers to the totality of the created universe and its divine mechanical laws.</note>*, that is: being, property, multiplicity, and motion.*

->*With this, the **Publisher** earnestly wishes the **Reader** all that is good.*<-

<meta>This page transitions from the table of contents to the main body of the work. The text begins with a moral exhortation, establishing the ethical prerequisites for the student of natural mysteries. A large brown stain in the center of the third paragraph partially obscures some words in the original Russian manuscript.</meta>

# THE KEY

## TO THE MYSTERIES OF NATURE.

---

->I.<-

### FIRST RULES

->For those wishing to dedicate themselves to the MYSTERIES of Nature.<-

Only he whose heart is kind is worthy to know the <term>mysteries of Nature</term><note>The term "mysteries" (original: таинства) here refers to the hidden laws of the physical and spiritual worlds, a common theme in 18th-century Hermetic philosophy.</note>; for he will use his knowledge for the benefit of humanity.

<term>Wisdom</term><note>original: "Премудрость" (Premudrost). This often refers to "Divine Wisdom" or Sophia, personified as a guiding celestial force.</note> is like the Sun, shining upon all people and warming all mortals: but, in order to feel the beneficence of its light and warmth, the body requires an <term>organization</term><note>In this context, "organization" refers to the physical and spiritual constitution or "tuning" of a person's character and senses.</note> that is in harmony with that light and warmth.

An evil person is unworthy to know the paths by which <term>Science</term><note>original: "Наука" (Nauka). In this era, "Science" referred not just to empirical study, but to the "Sacred Science" or the comprehensive understanding of God's creation.</note> leads to bliss; for that which in Nature is a blessing for humanity would, in his hands, become a curse. <note>original: "клятвою" (klyatvoyu). While this word can mean "oath," in this archaic context, it signifies a malediction or a spiritual burden.</note>

Fragrant flowers bloom in vain for one who has no sense of smell, and the crumbs from the Lord's table <note>original: "трапеза Господня" (trapeza Gospodnya). A biblical metaphor for spiritual nourishment or divine truth.</note> [the text breaks off here]

<summary>This page introduces the moral and spiritual requirements for those seeking to study the secrets of the natural world, emphasizing that wisdom requires a benevolent heart and a refined constitution.</summary>
<keywords>Nature, Mysteries, Divine Wisdom, morality, spiritual organization, Sacred Science, benevolence</keywords>

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page continues the introductory "First Rules" for seekers of natural mysteries. It completes a metaphor begun on the previous page regarding the "crumbs from the Lord's table" and shifts into the pedagogical philosophy of the text: that true knowledge of Nature cannot be taught by men, but must be experienced directly by a worthy seeker.</meta>

...do not fall for swine rooting in the mud.

It is a well-known rule in Nature that even the most healing plant, through ignorance of its full powers, can become a poison; just as it is the nature of the fly to blame for its being singed by a candle, as it does not know the properties of fire.

Let the reader take note of these principles; and consider that he who seeks pearls in a dung heap <note>original: "жемчужины въ навозной кучѣ" — a common alchemical and philosophical metaphor for finding spiritual value in base or overlooked places</note> must search for them for a long time.

One must not believe everything, yet one should not reject everything: to examine all things is the duty of the Wise Man; he is not blinded by outward brilliance, but searches for the truth.

### 2.
### ON THE HIDDEN MYSTERIES OF NATURE
### AND THE METHOD OF FINDING THEM.

No human being can perfectly teach the mysteries to another. Nature herself is the priestess in her own temple, and she <unclear>leads</unclear> <note>original: "вводитъ" — partially obscured by an ink smudge</note> into the <unclear>sanctuary</unclear> <note>original: "святилище" — partially obscured by an ink smudge</note> of that place only those who seek <unclear>her</unclear> <note>original: "ея" — partially obscured by an ink smudge</note> and those who are worthy of her. One who has learned any of her mysteries can point the way to another who desires to know these mysteries; but the student must walk the path himself.

<term>Nature (Натура): The personified forces of the physical and spiritual world.</term> <term>Mysteries (таинства): Hidden truths or "sacraments" of the natural world.</term> <term>Truth (истина): Spiritual and objective reality.</term> <term>The Wise Man (Мудрый): The enlightened seeker or Sage.</term> <term>Priestess (священница): A female minister of sacred rites, here representing Nature's role in initiation.</term>

--- Page 23 ---
<meta>This page continues the instructional "First Rules" for the seeker, moving from the responsibility of the student to the metaphor of Nature as a book. It follows the previous page’s discussion on the Teacher’s role as a guide rather than a source of truth.</meta>

It is not the <term>Teacher (Учитель): In this tradition, a spiritual guide or an elder adept who provides direction rather than direct knowledge.</term>’s fault if the student is lame or bow-legged and hobbles where one ought to walk straight; or if he is cross-eyed and blind, staring up at the sky while beautiful things lie right beneath his feet; or if he runs headlong and stumbles when the Teacher commands him to walk at a steady pace.

Do not hurry! The greatest mysteries of <term>Nature (Натура): The personified force of the physical and spiritual world, often viewed as the "second book" of God.</term> are close to us, and simplicity finds them more quickly than arrogance. Not everyone is worthy of the honor of being admitted into the temple of Nature; the Deity has arranged all things so wisely that the wicked cannot deceive Him.

A bird lives in the air, a fish in the water, and a man upon the earth; each <term>element (стихія): One of the four fundamental substances—earth, air, fire, or water—believed to constitute the world.</term> requires its own specific organization <note>original: "организаціи" — here referring to the physical or spiritual constitution of a being suited to its environment</note>. In the same way, the <term>Temple of Mysteries (храмъ таинствъ): A metaphorical space representing the inner sanctuary of hidden natural and spiritual laws.</term> requires a specific organization suited to that sphere in which the <term>Wisdom-lover (Мудролюбецъ): A literal translation of the Greek "philosopher," used here to denote a spiritual seeker.</term> desires to dwell. He who wishes to comprehend the mysteries of Nature must study her great book. This book is Nature herself. Happy is he who knows the letters in which this book is written; happier still is he who can assemble them into words; but happiest of all is he who knows how to read this book.

### 3.
### ON THE SUPERNATURAL AND THE MIRACULOUS.

The mediating power <note>original: "Посредствующая сила." This refers to the idea of Nature acting as the vehicle or "middle-man" for God's will in the physical world.</note> of the acting Divinity in this corporeal world is Nature. Whoever thinks to separate the Divinity from Nature, takes the soul away from the body. Everywhere where God acts mediately, the power of this His mediate action is Nature.

<gloss>Nature, Divinity, soul, discoveries, supernatural, miraculous</gloss>

<keywords>Nature, Divinity, spiritual seekers, mysteries, mediate action, supernatural</keywords>

--- Page 27 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from the previous section regarding the relationship between God and the physical world, specifically addressing the definitions of the supernatural and the mechanism of causality in Nature.</meta>

Weak human reason often calls that which it cannot explain in Nature <term>Nature (Natura): In this context, it refers to the physical world seen as an expression of Divine laws</term> "unnatural" or "supernatural"; and the supernatural lies more in the limitations of our understanding than in the essence of the thing itself.

In Nature there are Forces, Actions, and Consequences <note>original: "Силы, Дѣйствія и Слѣдствія." The author uses these terms in a semi-scientific, semi-mystical framework to describe how the universe operates.</note>: everything consists of them. Forces and actions are not bound by necessity; their necessity is only *relative*: but consequences are necessary; for consequences are the offspring of actions, and actions are the children of forces. By this proposition, the Providence of the Eternal <note>i.e., God’s divine guidance and foresight</note> and the freedom of human will are explained; for every force has countless actions, and every action has countless consequences. Force resides in the *entirety*, every action resides in the force, every consequence in the action: yet all together they constitute a single chain.

Without God, Nature is a dead entity. Nature is the herald of the Eternal; the organ proclaiming the Creator and connecting the Divinity with the corporeal world. Within her resides the active power of the Divinity for the benefit of the material world.

<gloss>Nature, Forces, Actions, Consequences, Providence, Divinity, reason</gloss>

<summary>The author argues that the "supernatural" is merely a label for what human reason cannot yet explain and details a hierarchy of causality—Forces, Actions, and Consequences—that allows for both Divine Providence and human free will.</summary>
<keywords>causality, free will, Providence, Nature, human reason, the Eternal, chain of being</keywords>

--- Page 28 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 7 regarding the relationship between God, Nature, and human understanding. It transitions from discussing the active force of the Divinity in the corporeal world to its force within the spiritual world.</meta>

the active force of the <term>Divinity</term> <note>original: Божество; refers to the essential nature or Godhead</note> for the benefit of the spiritual world is above Nature, and is the force of <term>essential likeness</term> <note>original: подобобытіе; a term suggesting a state of being similar to the divine essence, often used in mystical texts to describe the soul's relationship to God</note>. In this very force lies the foundation of constancy and immortality.

Every exception from the ordinary circle of action is also based on Nature; she does not change, but the variety of actions is the cause of various consequences. Different consequences may have the same force as their cause, but not the same action. Man, being by nature extremely lazy, always contents himself with the most proximate causes when explaining the phenomena he encounters. This is why in former times, everything extraordinary was attributed to <term>sorcery</term> <note>original: чародѣйство</note>; whereas in the present, people deny everything they do not understand. And so, the vice of former times was to believe everything; while the vice of the present is to reject everything they do not understand; for the wisdom of the majority of <term>Scholars</term> <note>the author uses this to refer to the academic establishment or "the learned" of the Enlightenment</note> is based on the opinions and accounts of others.

There is a great difference between thinking and saying what others have thought and said, and thinking and speaking from oneself. In our century, the Scholars have forgotten that theory owes its very existence to practice, and that Nature existed before rules.

<summary>This page argues that immortality is rooted in a divine "essential likeness" and critiques the intellectual laziness of both the past (superstition) and the present (skepticism), urging a return to original thought and practical experience over rigid rules.</summary>

### 4.

### ON THE INCLINATION TOWARD THE MIRACULOUS AND THE
### SUPERNATURAL.

A spirit incapable of valuing the true and the elegant is more easily attracted by the miraculous and the strange. The more ignorant and foolish a person is, the more rapidly their imagination is set in motion. For a lazy spirit, the most agreeable nourishment consists of miracles and curiosities; everything extraordinary holds a twofold charm for the ignorant. To the ignorant, even the most ordinary things are extraordinary.

The more a people possesses wondrous and strange fables: the more foolish, superstitious, and consequently ignorant that people is.

It is the duty of the industrious and the wise to penetrate the connection between events and phenomena, which requires reflection; whereas the lazy person does not reflect, and allows themselves all manner of daydreams, so long as they entertain their imagination.

An infant and a <term>savage</term> <note>original: дикой; a term used in the 18th and 19th centuries to refer to indigenous peoples or those perceived to be living in a "state of nature" before civilization</note> are satisfied with any answer, provided it tickles their <term>fantasy</term> <note>original: фантазія; here used to mean a flight of fancy or an irrational whim rather than creative imagination</note> in some way. The most pleasant thing for a man is that which touches him in a singular way, and

Man prefers to be deceived by his own expectations rather than to seek out authenticity. Little by little, he comes to recognize various blessings; in his soul, a desire is born to enjoy them. However, as this requires one to labor and—what is even more tedious—to reflect, while he prefers merely to desire; therefore, whatever promises him success in his desires with the least amount of effort is most pleasing to him. From this, it follows that a charlatan is more respected in society than a wise man; that he more quickly finds patronage among the rich and the noble; for the rich and the noble are, for the most part, lazy, and would wish to acquire everything without further labor.

### OBSTACLES ON THE PATH TO THE MYSTERIES OF NATURE.

Only the Wise Man <term>Mudryy — a person of true spiritual or philosophical insight</term> attains the mysteries of Nature. While testing the paths prescribed by others, he walks with his own pace.

Nature is like a friend; she has no secrets from those worthy of her friendship and her company. Curiosity, pride, vanity, a blind partiality for the opinions of others, and scholarly arrogance are but will-o'-the-wisps <note>original: *bludyashchiye ogni* — literally "wandering fires," a common metaphor for deceptive or misleading goals.</note> on the path to the mysteries of Nature. We often think ourselves close to them when we are, in fact, furthest away.

What are your eyes for? What are your ears for? What are your hands for? Is it not so that you yourself might see, yourself hear, and yourself touch? Why then do you wish to see with the eyes of others, hear with the ears of others, and touch with the hands of others? Whoever understands this premise will be in a position to explain many phenomena in Nature.

Our knowledge in **Physics** <note>original: "Физикѣ" (*Fizike*). In the 18th and early 19th centuries, "Physics" referred generally to "natural philosophy" or the study of all physical laws of the universe.</note> is still but toys compared to those things which are hidden from us; **Nature** <note>original: "Натура" (*Natura*).</note> sometimes imparts to man one of her secrets only to entice him into intimacy with her. She is like a beauty who occasionally reveals her minor charms, but diligently conceals her greatest ones.

Our **Philosophy** is still in its infancy; and what was called philosophy before was barbarism.

This likely refers to ancient sages, hermetic philosophers, or keepers of esoteric wisdom.</note> who preached: but the people did not understand them, and their writings were turned to laughter by the ignorant.

If one wishes to translate from one language to another, one must be skilled in both. — As long as man is...

### 6.

## A SMALL LIGHT FOR THOSE
## ON THE DARK PATH OF THE MYSTERIES OF NATURE.

In vast <term>physics collections</term><note>The original Russian word "магазин" (magazin) refers to a storehouse, repository, or a "cabinet" of scientific instruments and curiosities popular in the 18th century.</note> there are also philosophical toys; there, scholarly children amuse themselves with them, and kind mother Nature laughs, watching their puppet shows.

The wise man seeks true experiments in the <term>Temple of day</term> and in the <term>Sanctuary of night</term>. He learns <term>Chemistry</term><note>In this mystical context, chemistry refers to alchemy or the study of the fundamental "essences" of substances.</note> from the sun, the laws of motion from the air, the use of herbs from the beast, and medicine from different <term>types of air</term><note>Likely referring to "airs" or gases, which were a major focus of scientific discovery in the late 1700s.</note>. Morning and evening show him the various effects of herbs; the earth shows the power of stones and the glory of metals. Sunrise, noon, evening, and sunset are important <term>eras</term><note>The author uses "epokhi" (эпохи) to describe specific, potent moments in time or astronomical cycles.</note> that reveal many great mysteries.

When dew falls from the sky, when thunder shakes the earth, when on a cool evening lightning cleaves the air, when on a clear night the moon shines in the sky: then the hour arrives

Nature, Temple of day, Sanctuary of night, Chemistry, Sun, mysteries

...of Nature’s lessons; then she interprets her mysteries.

Ask yourself and investigate: what is a storm, and what power is hidden in the air? Break down snow into its component parts, and analyze the power of ice: then you will take a great step toward the mysteries of Nature.

Learn to distinguish fire from light; cast aside the misconception that suns burn <note>This likely refers to an esoteric belief that the Sun is a source of "cold light" or spiritual energy, and that physical heat is a result of that light's interaction with the Earth's atmosphere, rather than the Sun being a ball of fire.</note>; investigate what the <term>elements</term><note>In this context, the four classical elements: Earth, Water, Air, and Fire.</note> are, and what the human senses are, and you shall know the great mysteries.

Do not undertake the study of spirits until you have perfectly mastered the science of bodies; only after this, proceed to the study of spirits: then much will be clarified for you.

<margin>[Handwritten flourish]</margin>
You have an eye, an ear, a nose, a body; it is in your power to see what others do not see, to hear what others do not hear, and to feel what others do not feel: think on this well. This capacity exists in every person, to a greater or lesser degree. If you know this, then *premonitions and visions* will be understandable to you; they are based not on imagination, but truly exist in Nature.

Nature, mysteries, elements, spirits, bodies, premonitions, visions.

<meta>This page continues the instructional discourse from page 15, moving from the study of elements and spirits to the physical study of the human body and the vital forces within all matter.</meta>

->16<-

Do not neglect the knowledge of the human body. Ask yourself: what is blood? what is the nervous fluid? <note>original: "нервный сокъ" (nervous juice). A historical physiological concept suggesting a fluid or "animal spirit" flowed through the nerves to animate the body.</note> how does the electrical force act upon the body? how does the magnet? what is sleep? what are dreams? Consider this proposition: *in the corporeal world, everything is motion, and even rest itself is motion.* You will ask yourself: how is motion produced in Nature that is imperceptible to my coarse senses? And how to make this invisible motion perceptible to my senses? — Much of importance is contained within this.

What is life? what is death? What is called "to live," and what "to die"? Are life and death so opposite to one another? Is there death in nature, or is everything life?

What is the whole? what are the parts? Does a metal live? Does a stone live? and by what are their life forces distinguished?

# A CHAPTER NECESSARY FOR ANSWERING THE PREVIOUS QUESTIONS.

Unity is a great mystery of Nature, the goal of all things. The closer any being approaches Unity, the more perfect it becomes—a proposition that not everyone will fully comprehend.

All that lives in Nature feels within itself an attraction toward this; everything strives there upon the wheel of time, though some reach it sooner, and others later.

The great law of this goal is Love; it is the active force, the means to likeness of being, <note>original: "подобобытіе" (podobobytie). This is an archaic term implying a state of essential similarity or "consubstantiality"—the process by which a created being becomes like its Creator.</note> and the chain of unity. <note>The text likely refers to the "Great Chain of Being," a hierarchical structure of all matter and life.</note> It is the commandment of the Deity, imprinted upon the hearts of all men, and is the knot that binds all beings together. By it, the Wise man gives thanks for the power of spirit over spirit, for the hidden powers of the soul, and for the magnetic force. <note>In this era, "magnetic force" often referred to "animal magnetism," a believed invisible natural force that could influence the body and mind.</note> — Great God! What power still remains hidden even within this shell <note>original: "оболочка" (obolochka). A term used to describe the physical body as a mere casing or husk for the eternal soul.</note> of ours, announcing to us that we are Thy children! —

### THINGS OF WHICH FEW HAVE A CONCEPTION.

Man currently understands everything through the senses; yet the senses are limited and subject to deception, and consequently, our concepts are limited and deceptive. Because we rarely judge a thing by itself, but rather by our concepts of it, our errors arise from this.

The soul, however, being independent of the body, looks into the thing itself, and consequently knows the thing along with its actions and its consequences. Its sight is not a gradual examination, but a single instantaneous glance, in which power, action, and consequence constitute one whole.

Coarse organs are needed only for the manifestation of the inner power of life in creatures <note>The word *tvary* refers to all "created things," encompassing humans and animals alike.</note>, but <gloss>not</gloss> at all for their existence. Of all living creatures, the human <term>life-force</term><note>original: "жизни-сила" (*zhizni-sila*); a term common in 18th-century natural philosophy to describe the vital energy animating a physical body.</note> is the strongest and most active: but even here, there are degrees from the most foolish to the wisest.

## TO SEEK FOR ONESELF IS NOT THE SAME AS FOLLOWING OTHERS.

There are things very close to us which, nevertheless, remain unknown to the majority of people; partly because they do not know the <term>organ</term><note>In this context, "organ" refers to a spiritual faculty or "inner sense" rather than a biological body part.</note> through which these things are perceived; and partly because the nature of these things is such that they cannot find this organ even if they knew of it. Everything has a necessary cause by which a thing is what it is: with a change in these circumstances, the thing ceases to be what it was and begins to be something else.

Take flint and steel; strike one against the other, and you will strike fire: what could be simpler than this? And yet, for one who did not know of this, flint would be a useless thing.

What is simpler than the <term>electrical force</term><note>original: "электрической силы" (*elektricheskoy sily*). There are things even simpler which they also do not know: and they are as important as they are simple.

Anyone can find these things, for they are found in Nature: but one must seek them for oneself. To seek for oneself is no small science; the majority of people do not seek for themselves: they think they are seeking, but in truth, they only follow at the heels of others. Their guides are the common human leaders: prejudice, partiality toward someone else’s opinion, and their own arrogance.

### II.

## ON THE LANGUAGE WHICH HAS NO WORDS.

There is a language that has no
words, but is more excellent than all languages.

Thoughts are born within us from im-
pressions produced by objects
upon our senses. To receive these
impressions is called *to understand*; to
sort through them within ourselves is called *to think*;
to renew these conceptions is called
*to remember*; and to explain them
with words, *to speak*.

Every <term>creature</term><note>Russian: *tvar'*. In this context, it refers broadly to any "created thing" in nature, from minerals to animals, not just living beings in the modern sense.</note> explains itself,
and consequently *speaks*; and this language
corresponds to the perfection of the creature.
Animals produce sounds and modify...

<meta>This page continues the sentence from page 26 regarding how animals produce and modify sounds.</meta>

them according to their passions and needs; consequently, they also speak. Their expressions of joy and sorrow, love and anger, pleasure and suffering, are distinct in each. But these expressions are limited to feelings and passions alone, rather than representations of their <term>conceptions</term><note>Russian: *ponyatiya*. In this philosophical context, these are the mental images or intellectual ideas formed in the mind after encountering an object.</note>: man alone possesses the privilege of explaining with words the conceptions left in his soul by things.

The diversity of impressions produced a diversity of sensations within the soul. This diversity was the primary cause of the variety of signs. A different image is impressed upon my soul at the sight of a tree than at the sight of a rose: this is why humans chose different signs for them; for *language through signs preceded language through sounds*. If the coarse organization of the body did not prevent man from noticing the most subtle impressions in other men, he would have no need for words, which are merely expressions of impressions and not of the objects themselves—names for things, rather than the things themselves.

Between the spirit and the human heart, there is a close connection; their language is called *Contemplation*<note>Russian: *Sozertsaniye*. A key term in Russian philosophy and mysticism, meaning a direct, intuitive "beholding" of truth or the divine, beyond the use of analytical logic or words.</note>.

### On Reality in Obscure Sensations.

Everything is *reality*, or *authenticity*; even imagination is authenticity, only to a lesser degree.

In this corporeal world, man receives his knowledge through the senses. Every sense is capable of receiving impressions, and through these impressions, the images of things are communicated to our soul. These impressions remain for a longer or shorter time, depending on the nature of the organ or the impression itself.

Man feels even before he knows that he feels. Such sensations are *obscure sensations* <note>original: "темныя ощущенія" (temnyya oshchushcheniya). This refers to the philosophical concept of "obscure perceptions"—feelings or sensory inputs that reach the soul before the mind has consciously recognized or categorized them.</note>, and

I knew a certain man who, while carrying an important letter to the post office, dropped it along the way and was extremely distressed by this loss. During the night, he had a dream: it was as if some man, dressed in a gray kaftan <note>A traditional long, buttoned-up coat.</note>, on a specific street, picked up his packet and entered a nearby tavern <note>original: "трактиръ" (traktir). A traditional Russian inn or restaurant that served as a social hub.</note> with it. In the morning, he went to that tavern and indeed found that same man there, in that same kaftan, and received his lost letter from him.

It is not difficult to explain this occurrence. When he was walking to the post office, his thoughts were occupied with something else, and not the letter. While walking, he dropped it and saw right there the man who picked it up and went with it into that tavern. He saw all of this, but not clearly; it was obscure, because he was thinking of something entirely different. At night, however, when he found rest from those thoughts, that idea was renewed and became clear within him, and in his sleep, he remembered it all.

### FURTHER RULES.

Every state of the soul presupposes a certain indispensable disposition of the fibers and nerves, without which that state of the soul either cannot exist or would not be as it is. <note>In the 18th century, "fibers" were thought to be the fundamental building blocks of the body, acting like musical strings that "tuned" or "vibrated" to create different emotional or mental states.</note>

The disposition of the nerves and fibers depends on the temperament; the temperament on the <term>humors (соки)<gloss>literally "juices"; the bodily fluids (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile) believed to govern health and personality</gloss></term> and the circulation of the blood; and this on the variety of air, food, and so forth.

And so, he who knows how to change these things also knows how to tune the nerves in a person and bring the soul into a specific state. Upon this is based the knowledge of the actions of spirit upon spirit, soul upon soul. — The thinking person will understand this.

A few years ago, I happened to be in the company of a respectable man who, in our conversations, complained that his Superior could not stand him. I would spare nothing, he said, if only I could come into his favor and be rid of having to endure new insults every day. I, having spoken with him and inquired about everything...

I conducted another similar experiment with a man who could not tolerate roast pig. Having made an agreement with one of his best friends, we went to visit him, taking a piece of roast pig in our pocket. From the very first glance at us, he seemed dissatisfied, and finally even admitted to his friend that he did not know why, but that the friend was unbearable to him today. The next day we came to him again, but this time without the pig, and he was very glad to see us.

### 14.

## THE THEORY OF THE IMAGINATIVE FORCE WITH THE EXPLANATION OF CERTAIN PHENOMENA.

Knowledge of a present object is sensation <note>original: 'чувствованіе', meaning the act of feeling or perceiving through the senses</note>: but within us there is also another faculty which produces that same sensation in the absence of the object. This is *Imagination*.

Imagination is either *voluntary or involuntary*. The first is independent of the mechanism of the organs and their agitation <note>original: 'потрясенія', referring to a physical vibration or shock</note> caused by the circulating humors <note>original: 'соками', literally 'juices'—a reference to the medical theory of bodily humors</note>; the second consists of a specific state of the organs and a powerful agitation of the fibers <term>fibers: the basic structural filaments of the body's tissues, then believed to be the conduits of physical and mental sensation</term> through the blood.

And so, whatever sets the blood in motion can also set the imagination in motion, as we see examples of this in illnesses.

A quiet and calm circulation of the blood produces quiet and calm imaginings; a rapid and turbulent one produces frightening and terrible ones; a difficult circulation of the blo—

<vocab>imagination, sensation, voluntary, involuntary, mechanism of the organs, fibers, circulation of the blood</vocab>

<summary>This page defines imagination as the faculty that reproduces sensations in the absence of physical objects and distinguishes between voluntary imagination and involuntary imagination driven by blood circulation and bodily humors.</summary>
<keywords>Imagination, Sensation, Physiology, Blood Circulation, Humors, Psychology, Voluntary vs Involuntary</keywords>

--- Page 66 ---
<meta>Continuity from page 45: This page continues the discussion on how the physical circulation of the blood dictates emotional states and the "involuntary" imagination, specifically focusing on the four temperaments and the sensory inputs that influence the mind.</meta>

<margin><image-desc>Small hand-drawn flourish or ink mark resembling a letter 'p' or a loop</image-desc></margin>...od, and heavy dreams. Terror, fury, and anger are the consequences of turbulent blood:
<gloss>underlined: fear, timidity, and melancholy are the consequences</gloss> fear, timidity, and melancholy are the consequences of a stagnant circulation <note>original: 'остановочнаго обращенія', referring to blood flow that is sluggish or temporarily blocked</note> of the blood. The strength and weakness of these effects depend on one's temperament. Violently agitated blood in Sanguines <note>The Sanguine temperament was traditionally associated with a lively, talkative, and social nature</note> produces flightiness and recklessness; in Choleric types <note>The Choleric temperament was associated with an ambitious and energetic, but also aggressive or irritable nature</note>, it produces fury and rage. Stagnant circulation of the blood produces very ill effects in Phlegmatics and Melancholics.

The circulation of blood and humors <note>original: 'соковъ', literally 'juices'; in pre-modern medicine, these were the four vital fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—that determined health and character</note> depends largely on food, on the air we breathe in <note>original: 'глотаемъ', literally 'swallow'</note>, and on the elasticity of our body parts.

The imagination can be acted upon through all five senses: through food, incense <note>original: 'куренія', referring to medicinal vapors or aromatic smoke used to alter one's state</note>, sounds, touch, and the presentation of objects to the sight that bring the fantasy into involuntary motion. Memory is the companion of imagination. Similar images excite similar ones within us. Once at a ball, I was asked by a young man in love to show him his mistress <note>The original Russian "любовница" (lyubovnitsa) in this period typically referred to a sweetheart or the lady of one's affections, rather than necessarily implying an illicit relationship.</note>. Then I ordered a glass of water to be brought to me and went with it into another room, asking him to bring his beloved to mind as vividly as possible. Meanwhile, I used wax to stick the portrait I had chosen to my palm and intentionally stirred the water so that the image would not be reflected in it too quickly or too clearly. "Sit down," I told the lover, "in my place; cross your arms as I do; place them against your forehead, and look intently into the water." As soon as the water settled and the portrait began to be reflected in it from my palm, it seemed to his heated imagination that he truly saw his beloved. This man frequently visited me afterward and asked for the same thing, and through this action, I provided him with many pleasant hours.

— — — And so there truly are such things in Nature <note>original: "Натурѣ" (Natura). In the 18th century, Nature was often personified as a vast, divinely ordered system containing hidden powers.</note> of which our philosophy <note>A likely allusion to Shakespeare’s *Hamlet*: "There are more things in heaven and earth, Horatio, than are dreamt of in your philosophy."</note> does not even conceive. The Divinity has hidden much from mortals, and the Eternal has sealed many mysteries of Nature from us with an unbreakable seal. Not everything is fantasy; much, perhaps, is the very truth: for remember, my dear friend, that once immeasurable seas separated humans from one another; Europeans did not know the Americans <note>Referring to the indigenous peoples of the Americas; the author uses the "discovery" of the New World as a metaphor for the potential discovery of spiritual realms.</note>. Swedenborg <note>Emanuel Swedenborg (1688–1772), a world-renowned Swedish scientist and philosopher who later became a mystic, claiming he could travel to the spirit world.</note> and Falck <note>Likely referring to the "Baal Shem of London," Hayyim Samuel Jacob Falk (c. 1708–1782), a famous alchemist and kabbalist who fascinated European high society.</note> were certainly not rogues, and yet their lives remain incomprehensible to us; and perhaps will remain incomprehensible to many until the grapes ripen and the time comes to press wine from them <note>An allegorical way of saying that the truth will only be revealed when the time is right or humanity is ready.</note>. <meta>This page continues the author's discussion from page 62 regarding experiments on how external physical sensations can influence the imagination and shape the content of dreams.</meta>

[...formation] of various images. Once I said to one person: "Today you saw a rose in your dream"; to another: "In your dream you walked beneath a linden tree"; to a third: "You saw a cat."

They all admitted that this was indeed the case, fell into a state of wonder, and began to press me with questions: how did I know this? This effect, however, was the natural consequence of the method I had employed. For the first person, I had sprinkled the pillow on the bed with rose water; for the second, with water from linden blossoms; and for the third, with cat urine.

But it is necessary that these individuals do not know of it; moreover, the pillows must be sprayed only lightly, so that the scent remains faint. During sleep, the imagination of a person resting in the presence of the scent of a rose—which sets their fibers <term>fibers: In 18th-century physiology, 'fibers' were thought to be the basic structural units of the nerves and muscles that vibrated or moved in response to sensory stimuli</term> into motion—will present them with a rose, and so on. I have performed many experiments of this kind, and they rarely failed me; only in people moved by strong passions, in the drunk, or those over-satiated with food, does this produce no effect. This is natural; for the matter <note>original: "матерія" — likely referring to the bodily humors or fluids common in early medical theory</note> circulating in the body does not allow the fibers the opportunity for delicate sensation. This is confirmed...

### 15.

## CHAPTER ON THE SOUL.

The being within us that feels, thinks, wills, and acts is the soul.

The body in itself does not feel, does not will, and does not act: thus, the being that feels, acts, and wills is distinct from the body.

No created spirit penetrates into the interior of its nature; happy is he to whom she <note>The author uses the feminine "she" as the Russian word for soul, *dusha*, is feminine.</note> shows her outward veil.

The soul is not <term>composite</term><note>In philosophical terms, "not composite" means the soul is a single, simple substance rather than something made of parts that can be broken down.</note>, not corporeal, and is indivisible.

The soul possesses the power to feel, to think, and to act. It does not always feel, think, or act; but the possibility of feeling, thinking, and acting is always found within it.

---

<note>This paragraph continues a footnote from page 64 regarding the use of technology to simulate spiritual phenomena.</note>
...[instru]ments and machines, about which Hume, Funk, Marcius, and Gall have written so much, and of which we have seen examples in part in the <term>phantasmagoria</term><note>A form of theater using "magic lanterns" to project frightening images, such as skeletons or ghosts, popular in the late 18th century.</note> of Mr. Robertson <note>Étienne-Gaspard Robert (1763–1837), known as "Robertson," was a famous Belgian physicist and stage magician known for his "Fantasmagorie" shows.</note>: but as this does not properly belong to the subject of this book, we omit them, proceeding directly to the matter following this. *Translator's Note.*

--- Page 86 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of the soul, moving from its definition as an indivisible entity to the metaphysical and physiological mechanics of how it interacts with the physical body. It bridges the gap between Enlightenment-era vitalism and early nervous system studies.</meta>

The soul and the body are closely linked to one another. From this follows the question: how can entities, so essentially different from one another, be so closely united? They are united through a medium, and this medium consists of a knot <note>The author uses "knot" (Russian: *uzel*) to describe a nexus or "vinculum," a common philosophical concept used to explain the bond between mind and matter.</note>, which partly borrows from the immaterial simplicity of the soul, and partly from the material composition of the body.

Air and fire constitute the ethereal body, which the Ancients called the *body of the soul*, and the moderns <note>Referring to contemporary philosophers of the late 18th or early 19th century.</note> call the *schema of perception* <note>original: "schema perceptionis," a Latin term likely used here to describe the conceptual framework or "blueprint" through which the soul perceives the material world.</note>.

Through this ethereal, fiery shell, the soul acts upon the finest nerve fibers; and these transmit its action to the coarser and visible nervous system.

The action of the soul upon the body is like a high-pitched tone, which, descending lower and lower, finally becomes dense.

The action of the body upon the soul is like the lowest tone, which, rising higher and higher, is finally lost in the most delicate.

Such is the gradation in sensations as well. In what relation the very last shade of the darkest color

<keywords>soul-body connection, ethereal body, schema perceptionis, nervous system, vitalism, musical analogy, gradation of sensations</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 66 regarding the metaphysical connection between the soul and the body, specifically completing the analogy of light and color shades as a parallel to the soul’s interaction with the physical world.</meta>

to the very first shade of the brightest: in such a relation is the action of bodily objects on the concepts of the soul.

And in what relation the very first shade of the brightest color stands to the very last of the darkest; in such is the action of the soul on the body.

The transition from the soul to the body is a transition from light to twilight: the transition from the body to the soul is a transition from twilight to light. Nature <note>The author personifies Nature here as a teacher or guide.</note> shows us this truth in the way she presents objects to the eye, and the way the eye sees them.

The line, going from the eye to the object, increases: a point becomes a line, and the line gradually expands into the volume of a body.

The line from a bodily object to the eye gradually decreases, and, diminishing in a physical manner, arrives at a single point. <note>This section describes the "cone of vision" in classical optics as a metaphor for how physical reality is concentrated into the singular point of the soul's perception.</note>

Everywhere the greatness of the Deity is visible—an indication of immortality—a call to reverence. — —

### 16.
## IMMORTALITY.

In nature everything lives, there is nothing dead: that which we call

--- Page 88 ---
<meta>This page continues from page 67, completing the definition of death as a transition rather than an end, following the section titled "IMMORTALITY."</meta>

death, is a transition from life to life.

A dead being is something impossible in nature — a fable. Every destruction is a transition to a higher <note>The word "вышней" (vyshney) implies a more elevated or celestial state of being.</note> life.

Every creature <note>original: "тварь" (tvar) — a term used in the 18th century to describe any created being or organism, without the modern negative connotation.</note> has its own life: only the forces of life act differently; and this depends on the constituent parts of the body and on the manner of their composition. The inner power of creatures is the force of life; life is the mode and manner by which the inner power manifests itself within the coarse organs.

Everything strives toward perfection — toward its destiny <note>The author uses "назначеніе" (naznacheniye), suggesting a divinely appointed purpose or "calling" for every element of creation.</note> — with the only difference being that one reaches it sooner, another later. There are no exceptions to this rule; one only encounters obstacles within matter. Everything has its own defined paths — its own world — its own circle of activity.

Within this very world are found thousands of worlds — only they are not as perfect; and in them, too, life, growth, and transition hold sway; and in them, there is a likeness <note>Likeness (подобіе) refers to the hermetic and philosophical principle that the macrocosm is reflected in the microcosm.</note>.

<margin>
Melancholy and Sublime

Survey the world of stones and metals, the world of plants, the world of animals. Look at the pearl by the seashore, at the oyster in its shell; marvel at the first formation of a living <term>creature<note>Original: "tvar." In this period, this term referred to any "created thing" in God's universe, lacking the modern derogatory meaning.</note></term>, and bow before the Eternal.

Everything in Nature constitutes an infinite chain; everything in Nature is connected to one another. All creation is a single whole, in which everything is arranged according to a single blueprint; in everything there is symmetry, proportion, measure, number, and weight; and there is nothing that was not included in the universal plan of the Deity.

In the vast multitude of creatures, each has its assigned nature, place, time, purpose, perfection, strength, and boundaries. Bodies are destroyed, but not annihilated; they only change their form; their role is to continue acting upon the corporeal world <note>The author suggests that physical matter stays within the cycle of the physical "corporeal" world even after death.</note>.

The human spirit, upon its release from the body, no longer acts upon this corporeal world; its destiny in the chain of things is a further journey—consequently, a transition into the spiritual world.

Man, the noblest <term>creature<note>Original: "tvar." In the 18th-century context, this refers to any created being within God's hierarchy, rather than the modern sense of an animal.</note></term> in this corporeal world, is already joined even here to a higher being, the one nearest to him in the gradual continuation of the chain.

Gold is pure only when it leaves behind all the dross <note>Original: "vygarki," referring to the waste or impurities left over after smelting metal.</note>. A flower bulb ceases to be a bulb when its inner strength unfolds into a flower. Everything announces immortality to man: yet he often judges the most perfect things by the imperfect. O man! how limited is your insight! — —

Each link acts upon the one following it, and upon the one preceding it: either downward or upward.

Man consists of body and soul. By the body, he belongs to the physical world; by the soul, to the spiritual. From this arises a dual inclination, or the **law of the flesh**, warring against the **law of the spirit**. <note>A reference to the Pauline theology found in the New Testament (Romans 7:23), describing the moral struggle between physical impulses and spiritual ideals.</note>

From this also comes that incomprehensible riddle, that man now rises to the level of an **Angel**, now degrades himself to that of a **worm**. <note>This imagery was a hallmark of 18th-century Russian thought, famously captured in Gavriil Derzhavin's "Ode to God": "I am a king, I am a slave, I am a worm, I am God!" The closer man approaches his future destiny <note>original: "назначенію" (appointment, calling, or destination)</note>, the more perfect he becomes. **God is Love** — the creation of infinite Love is a constant action.

It is not the soul’s fault that the tones of harmony do not reach it, but rather the disharmony of our organ—the *ear*.

The power of **Imagination**, which consists solely in bringing the nerves into the same state of vibration they were in when physical objects acted upon them, can be brought to such a level of tension that it becomes, in itself, a true reality. — Of course, this is still a mystery for many, but not for everyone.

Thus, it is possible to both see and hear directly through the movements of the **finer organization** <note>Likely referring to the "subtle body" or spiritual nervous system, as opposed to the physical body.</note>, without the mediation of the **coarser organization** <note>The physical body and its sense organs.</note>. For the thinking person, this is sufficient; — let us proceed further.

The thought of higher beings <note>original: "вышнихъ существъ" — referring to celestial or spiritual entities.</note> exceeds human thought in purity and, through the guidance of the soul, enters clearly into the finest organization of the thinking person. It becomes a human thought, yet it remains pure, like a sunbeam gilding the peaks of the mountains at clear noon.

A human, in speaking, produces tones, or sounds, which are formed into words, enter the ear, and reach the waiting soul. But higher creatures <note>original: "твари" — used here in the archaic sense of "created beings," not as a pejorative.</note> do not speak with us in this way. The tone of their voice...

The sensation of approach is the touch of the soul—a feeling through the most delicate organization, by which the soul senses the presence of a thing that, relative to the body, is still distant and in the future.

If someone receives a wound to their body: even though it heals, they will always feel the approach of a change in weather in that part, like a living barometer. The reason for this lies in the weakness of the nerves in that part,

### ON VISIONS HAVING THEIR FOUNDATION IN THE DECEPTION OF THE SENSES.

We have spoken of visions based on soulful sensation and the finest organization <note>This refers to the previous chapter's discussion on how highly sensitive physical or spiritual constitutions might perceive things beyond normal sight.</note>: now let us speak of visions based on the deception of the senses.

Among these, I include various phenomena explained by the rules of Optics and Catoptrics <term>Catoptrics: the study of light reflection through mirrors, often used in the 18th century to create "ghostly" illusions or stage magic</term>. To these, I attribute the wondrous appearance of human-like figures in the darkness, the vision of one’s own self <note>This phenomenon, known as autoscopy or seeing one's "doppelgänger," is categorized here as a physical optical error rather than a supernatural event.</note>, and the artificial appearance of living persons according to the rules of Optics.

<summary>This page concludes the philosophical discussion on spiritual love as a bridge to the divine and begins a new chapter on optical illusions. The author distinguishes between genuine spiritual perceptions and "visions" caused by physical deceptions of the eyes or mirrors.</summary>

<keywords>spiritual love, likeness to Divinity, eternal unions, optical illusions, Catoptrics, doppelgänger, scientific explanation of visions</keywords>

--- Page 119 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from the previous section regarding "Visions Rooted in the Deception of the Senses," specifically focusing on how natural atmospheric conditions like fog and smoke create optical illusions that may be mistaken for supernatural apparitions.</meta>

Regarding the first of these, it must be noted that smoke and fog are capable of reflecting light and can present the shadows of forms. In the autumn, during the evening, fog hovers over rivers, marshes, ponds, and damp places. When approaching such a location—especially when walking toward the east while the setting sun illuminates you from behind—it often happens that the shadow of the person walking is reflected in the fog. It then appears as if a dark <term>muzhik<gloss>A common Russian term for a peasant man; in this context, it refers to a looming, indistinct human figure.</gloss></term> is walking directly toward you.

This phenomenon can also be observed indoors, if a room is filled with enough smoke that it swirls in columns. If several people enter this room and the last person carries a candle, the shadows of those in front will be reflected in the smoke. This makes it appear as if several dark men are standing in the room. This illusion will vanish as soon as the person who entered first takes the candle; for the cause of the shadow's reflection will naturally disappear.

Water vapors form a mirror-like surface; and if there is a dark object behind them, one can see themselves reflected within them.

<summary>This page explains how smoke and fog act as reflective surfaces, creating optical illusions where a person's own shadow appears as a "dark peasant" or mysterious figure. The author provides a rationalist explanation for these "visions" using the principles of light and reflection.</summary>
<keywords>optical illusions, reflection, atmospheric phenomena, shadows, fog, smoke, rationalism, sensory deception</keywords>

--- Page 120 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of atmospheric reflections and optical illusions, moving from outdoor phenomena like fog to indoor occurrences involving stagnant air and vapors in enclosed spaces.</meta>

It often happens that chests filled with clothing, after standing for a long time in a cellar or in a damp place, absorb such vapors; if someone should happen to open them unexpectedly, it occurs that they see themselves through the reflection of these vapors. The image vanishes immediately, because the incoming air upon the opening of the chest disperses the vapors and, consequently, destroys the <term>mirror-like surface<gloss>A phenomenon where a dense layer of moisture or gas acts as a reflective medium, similar to a glass mirror.</gloss></term>. In dense forests, where there are marshes and damp places, the same may occur, especially on a clear and still day.

A certain reliable person told me that once, upon opening a chest of clothes, he saw his own image, which so frightened him that he fell unconscious. <note>In the 18th and 19th centuries, fainting from "nervous shock" was a common trope in accounts of supposedly supernatural encounters.</note> It is probable that this occurred in the manner described above.

### 18.

## VISIONS WHICH HAVE THEIR BASIS IN BODILY DISEASES AND IN THE APPROACH OF DEATH.

In this physical world, man always depends on <term>Nature<note>original: "Natura." In this context, it refers to the physical world and the biological laws governing human existence.</note></term>. In vain does the proud man imagine himself a separate being. The <term>Elements<note>original: "Stikhii." This refers to the classical elements—earth, air, fire, and water—which were traditionally believed to sustain life and influence the human temperament.</note></term> that sustain his life act upon both his spirit and his body, in both healthy and diseased states.

One need only, says <note>Antoine-Joseph Pernety (1716–1796) was a French writer and alchemist. He often wrote about the intersection of the physical and the spiritual.</note>Pernety, turn one’s eyes to the objects closest to us to be convinced that everything surrounding, nourishing, and growing our body acts upon it incessantly and can produce great changes in the soul, with which it is so closely connected.

It was mentioned above how great the power of imagination is over man: diseases bear witness to the kinds of fantasies that can be produced by corrupted <term>bile<note>A reference to "humoralism," an ancient medical system where health was determined by the balance of four bodily fluids: blood, phlegm, black bile, and yellow bile.</note></term>, thickened blood, and the obstructed circulation of humors.

<vocab>Nature, Elements, Pernety, imagination, bile, blood, disease</vocab>

<summary>This page argues that human visions are often not supernatural, but rather the result of physical ailments and the influence of the natural world on the human body and soul.</summary>
<keywords>Nature, Pernety, humoral medicine, imagination, pathology of visions, elements, physiological psychology</keywords>

--- Page 124 ---
<meta>This page continues the exploration of visions rooted in physical pathology, transitioning from the general influence of "humors" discussed on the previous page to the specific mechanics of the nervous system and the "fibers" of the human body.</meta>

And so there are visions that have their basis solely in diseases. They arise from the fatigue or the strain of the <term>nerves<note>In 18th-century medical theory, nerves were often viewed as physical conduits or strings that could become "overstretched" or "lax," affecting a person's mental state.</note></term>.

There are also such states where only certain <term>fibers<note>original: "fibry." Early modern physiology held that the body was composed of microscopic fibers; their vibration or tension was thought to facilitate sensation and thought.</note></term> of the finest organization can be set in motion, while all others are incapable of being stimulated. From such a state arise *unconsciousness, dullness, and stupidity*.

There are, again, states in which a single fiber is constantly strained and no longer contracts as it should according to its elastic property. From this arises *madness*, in which a person is always occupied with a single thought <note>This describes what would later be called "monomania," a form of partial insanity centered on one specific delusion or preoccupation.</note>.

By way of comparison, I cannot better explain this state than by likening it to an <term>organ<note>original: "organam." The author refers to the musical pipe organ to illustrate how mechanical failure in the body leads to a loss of "harmony" in the mind.</note></term>. As soon as the springs by which air is blown into the pipes lose their elasticity, you will hear no tone at all; but if one spring is too tightly strained, only one tone will be heard continually: whereby, consequently, the entire harmony is destroyed.

<summary>The author explains that mental impairments and "visions" are mechanical failures of the body's fibers and nerves, using a musical organ as a metaphor for the loss of mental harmony.</summary>
<keywords>visions, nerves, fibers, madness, physiology, organ metaphor, harmony, mental health</keywords>

--- Page 125 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 104 regarding the physiological basis of mental states, specifically following the metaphor of the organ where over-tension or lack of tension in the "nervous fibers" dictates the type of mental affliction.</meta>

The latter <note>referring to the state of insanity or preoccupation with a single thought mentioned on the previous page</note> can very easily happen to children and people of a delicate nervous constitution, especially during a severe fright.

Everything that overstrains the nerves—every strong passion—can, by the nature of the organism, produce a certain degree of madness <note>original: *сумасшествія* (sumasshestviya)</note>.

Proud and headstrong individuals are especially prone to this; for their stubbornness is already proof of their natural inclination toward folly <note>original: *сумасбродству* (sumasbrodstvu) — literally "wandering of the mind," implying erratic or extravagant behavior</note>.

Kind, gentle characters are rarely subject to folly; instead, they are more likely to fall into stupidity or imbecility <note>original: *безуміе* (bezumiye)</note>, which differs from madness in that folly is based on overstrained nerves, while stupidity is based on nerves that have become too slack.

Therefore, the stupid or the simple-minded <note>original: *полоумные* (poloumnye), literally "half-minded"</note> do not remember much; for in them, one idea suppresses the other, and not a single one is properly formed. Their condition is pitiable, and consequently, Doctors <term>Doctors|The author uses the capitalized "Доктора," likely referring to the emerging medical profession of the late 18th century.</term> make a great mistake when they treat the simple-minded in the same way as the mad.

When, during an illness, people of weak sight become clear-eyed, and those hard of hearing <note>original: *крѣпкіе на ухо* (krepkie na ukho), literally "tough of ear"</note> begin to hear easily: this is a certain sign of death. There are people who recognize the hour of their own death. This occurs through the soul’s ability to know the measure of its own bodily strengths.

But for this, a particular organization <note>original: *организація* (organizatsiya) — the specific structural makeup of an individual's body and mind</note> is also required, which is not common to everyone. One who knows the chain and the spring of a watch can easily determine the time when it will run down. Anyone is capable of determining, by sight alone, when the sand will flow out of an hourglass by measuring the time by the fallen sand; in exactly the same way, people, through an internal sense, feel the exhaustion of their machine <note>original: *машины* (mashiny) — a common Enlightenment-era metaphor viewing the human body as a mechanical system</note>, and can therefore determine their own end.

Corrupted vapors are no longer suited for the same circulation they possessed when the body was in a healthy state; they lose their volatility <note>The ability of a substance to evaporate or turn into gas.</note>, becoming coarser and heavier, harder to separate from the body, and more prone to sticking together. They become more visible, and therefore, at the slightest reflection, the image of a person appears within their own exhalations, as if on a mirror-like surface, which was mentioned above.

<margin>Prediction</margin>

There is also a circulation of our exhalations in the air, which pertains to the preservation of the living body <term>animal body: in 18th and 19th-century science, "animal" (zhivotnoye) referred to any living creature with a "soul" or life force, including humans, as opposed to plants or minerals</term>.

Many illnesses arise from the corruption of these exhalations in the air, yet physicians treat the body instead of striving to improve the atmosphere surrounding it.

### 19.

## ON PREDICTIONS AND PROPHECIES.

To predict is defined as determining future occurrences before they happen.

People measure time by the impressions things make on the senses: from this arise the past, the present, and the future, which are based on the changing state of our senses.

Our senses are limited: the understanding of things, which enters the soul by means of a most delicate organization <note>This refers to the "organic structure" of the body, specifically the nervous system and sensory apparatus, viewed as a refined machine for the soul's use.</note>, proceeds gradually. We feel that we cannot suddenly encompass various concepts within ourselves, but only one idea after another.

Time and distance are attributes of the corporeal world; for they are changes to which the spiritual world is not subject.

In the spiritual world, all that is possible is always present.

The human spirit, , while it languishes in corporeal bonds, perceives things only gradually: its gaze beco- <note>The text cuts off mid-word; in the original Russian, "stano-" likely completes as "stanovitsya," meaning "becomes."</note>

<summary>The author defines prediction and explains that while humans perceive time linearly due to sensory limitations, the spiritual world exists in a constant "present" where time and distance do not apply.</summary>
<keywords>prediction, prophecy, metaphysics, senses, spiritual world, corporeal world, nature of time</keywords>

--- Page 129 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical discourse from page 108 regarding the nature of the spirit, time, and the human inability to perceive the future due to physical limitations.</meta>

...becomes more perfect when it is freed from these bonds. <note>Referring to the "bodily bonds" mentioned on the previous page which limit the human spirit.</note>

If we were to rise high above our earthly globe, we would always see the sun; but here, it does not always remain with us. This transience <note>original: преходимость; the quality of passing away or being temporary</note> of the sun, which constitutes our day, resides not in the essence of the sun itself, but in the nature of the earth revolving around the sun. From this movement come the rising and setting of the sun, noon and midnight. The sun is transient only relatively, and not in its own essence.

Eternity has neither time, nor space, nor beginning, nor end. Therefore, the Pre-Eternal One <note>original: Превѣчный; a traditional title for God, meaning "He who existed before all ages"</note> sees all the future by His very nature, for to Him, everything is the present.

The limitation of our senses prevents us from seeing the future; for our sensations are based on impressions in our physical organs, which, being stimulated only gradually, have their limits.

Man judges the future only by comparing the present with the past; he is permitted to know the future...

Speaking of the foreknowledge of the future, I wish to pose a question which I once posed to myself:
