<meta>This page continues the author's exploration into the possibility of foretelling the future through scientific or "natural" means, rather than purely mystical ones. The previous page ended with a question about whether future events can be foreseen using physical principles.</meta>

Is it not possible, according to the physical laws accepted until now, to find a way to accurately predict the future?

I examined this question for a long time; finally, I reached a point where I can give you a sealed note; keep it wherever you like, and at the designated time, unseal it: you will find written in it something that, perhaps, no person could have known at the time the note was written. To one gentleman here I handed three sealed notes, in the first of which was predicted the successful birth of a Prince; in the second, the failure of an experiment with an <term>air balloon<note>The original Russian is "воздушнымъ шаромъ." This likely refers to the hot air balloon experiments popular in the late 18th century.</note></term>; in the third, the unexpected arrival of a certain person. These notes were given to him by me several months prior; during all this time they lay sealed and locked; on the appointed days they were opened by him personally: and everything was found to be predicted exactly as it happened. Now I ask the <term>Investigators of Nature<note>Original: "Испытателей Натуры." A common archaic term for natural philosophers or scientists who study the laws of the physical world.</note></term>: in what manner did I learn this?

For example: I might have written: *An unexpected lawsuit will force the aunt*

I declare this beforehand so that those wishing to learn this secret are not misled. I do not, however, wish to publish this rare invention for many reasons. Rare physical secrets are not for everyone; to share them broadly would be to profane them. ## On Sleep and Drowsiness, and the State of Somnambulists and Hysterics.

Our concepts in this corporeal <note>Physical or bodily</note> world depend upon the senses; they convey objects to the soul.

A person whose senses are sharp <note>Original: "unblunted"</note> can always see, smell, touch, and hear.

It sometimes happens that he sees, smells, hears, and touches all at the same time: but such an action of objects upon all the senses at once does not provide a clear perception, but produces a clouded sensation.

<vocab>Nature, sleep, drowsiness, Somnambulists <note>Sleepwalkers</note>, Hysterics, senses, soul, sensation, grace</vocab>

<summary>The author concludes his philosophical questions with a poem about the necessity of diligent labor in scientific discovery before transitioning to a study of human senses and states of consciousness like sleep and somnambulism.</summary>
<keywords>natural philosophy, labor vs curiosity, senses, soul, sleep, somnambulism, hysteria, sensory perception</keywords>

--- Page 140 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion initiated on page 119 regarding how the soul perceives the physical world through the senses, moving from general perception to the effects of sensory focus and overload.</meta>

The more the soul occupies itself with a single object, the more active those sensory organs become through which that object reaches the soul. For example, when listening intently to a story, we become, so to speak, "all ears"; we neither see nor feel anything else. In the same way, we are sometimes focused entirely through the eyes.

However, when all our senses are suddenly stimulated by many objects at once, we become, as it were, stunned <note>The Russian word *оглушены* literally means "deafened," but here describes a state of being overwhelmed by sensory input.</note>.

If such an impact <note>Original: *потрясеніе*, meaning a shaking or profound shock</note> produced by objects upon the sensory organs is intense, the soul falls into a state of confusion.

But if the impact is gentle, it produces within us a kind of pleasant daze. The soul feels something inexplicable, which at times casts us into such a delightful drowsiness <note>Original: *усыпленіе*, a state of falling asleep or being lulled into a trance</note> that it cannot be explained.

It was into this very state that the famous London doctor, *Graham* <note>James Graham (1745–1794) was a Scottish doctor famous for his "Temple of Health" in London, where he used electricity, music, and "ethereal spirits" to treat patients.</note>, sought to lead [his patients]; a state which is nothing other than a restored equilibrium of the sens[es]— <note>The text cuts off mid-word (*чув-* for *чувствъ*) at the bottom of the page.</note>

When all the senses are brought into a balanced state, this condition strengthens them, and they acquire new vigor and activity.

The merciful Deity has granted us this beneficial state in sleep. Nature then raises gentle, moisturizing vapors to the head and irrigates the tense fibers <term>fibers: in 18th-century medicine, "fibers" were considered the fundamental structural components of all tissues, which needed to be kept at a proper tension</term> with them. From this irrigation, they lose some of their usual tension; they relax slightly, and thus man sinks into sleep.

As are the humors <term>juices: (Russian: *soki*) referring to the bodily fluids or "humors" that were believed to govern a person's health and temperament</term> in the body, so is the sleep. Thick, sticky fluids produce a deep and heavy sleep; thin and pure ones produce a light and pleasant one.

This is why certain drinks <note>likely referring to alcoholic spirits or "strong waters"</note> hinder peaceful sleep; for they dry out the nerves and fibers and deprive them of the gentle moisture with which they are irrigated during sleep.

Just as the dew, falling from the sky in the evening, moistens the flowers and gives rest to their vessels <note>original: "сосудцамъ," referring to the small conduits or veins within a plant</note>, so that with the coming morning they might again be capable of...

What dew is for plants, sleep is for man in the economy <term>economy: (Russian: *ekonomiya*) the internal management, distribution of resources, and systematic operation of the physical body</term> of this corporeal world.

Drowsiness differs from sleep in the degree of tension in the nerves.

In drowsiness, objects still act upon the senses; but as the soul is at rest and does not properly command any of the senses, it does not retain the impressions produced in them.

A light zephyr <note>original: "зефиръ," a gentle west wind, often personified in Enlightenment-era poetry to represent a soft, cooling breeze</note> in spring sways only the little flowers, and a small boat glides over the ripples of a smooth lake: so is falling asleep sweet to weary limbs.

How pleasant it is to drowse in a garden, when a quiet breeze lulls the senses, when the sound of a murmuring brook echoes in the ears, and the fragrance of flowers touches the sense of smell!—

Hearing, smell, taste, and sight are merely modifications of touch. I see, hear, smell, and taste only when something touches me and sets in motion the nerves of the organs of sight, hearing, smell, or taste; in other words, when I touch. To touch with the eyes is called seeing; to touch with the ear, hearing; to touch with the nose, smelling; to touch with the tongue and palate, tasting.

A change in the particles touching my organs also changes sight, hearing, smell, and taste.

Particles contrary to our organization excite unpleasant impressions in our senses; while those that harmonize with us create pleasant ones.

Perhaps some will say: why then do we need these coarse senses, when the inner sense is superior to them in both power and activity?

I answer: every body is adapted to that sphere in which it lives; every organ to the property of the whole.

If this were otherwise, the connection between the body and the soul, and the soul and the body, would be severed. Our very organization <note>original: 'организація' — in this period, the term referred to the physical structure or biological makeup of a living being</note> will answer this question for us; for the structure of all our sensory organs—if I may dare to say so—is like a **filtering machine**, through which all coarse particles are constantly filtered, until they pass from the most delicate organization into unity and simplicity, into spirit, and from spirit into the soul.

<margin><unclear>faint pencil note</unclear></margin>
Since man is the highest creature of this bodily organization, and the lowest in the chain of the spiritual world: it can easily be concluded that incredible abilities are still contained here within his soul; for our current life is nothing other than a **preparatory state** for the future one.

And so, the more this inner power of the soul reveals itself, the more incomprehensible the phenomena produced by this revelation will be to us; for we judge not by the nature <note>original: 'естество' — meaning the essence or inherent nature of a thing</note> of the soul, but by the nature of the body.

22.

## ASSUMPTIONS NECESSARY FOR THE EXPLANATION OF SOMNAMBULISM.

There exists in Nature a fluid substance, common to all things, which the Ancients called the *first matter*; and modern philosophers call the *universal fluid* <note>original French: "fluide universel"</note>.

All things corporeal are contained within this fluid.

Its modifications are countless.

It is the cause of forms, and the foundation of a thousand changes.

Within it resides the productive power; it is <term>magnetism</term> <note>In this context, "animal magnetism" or Mesmerism, a hypothesized vital force or invisible natural force possessed by all living things</note>, and electricity, and the matter of heat, and light, and so forth.

The <term>elements</term> <note>Referring to the classical four elements: earth, air, fire, and water</note> are its first outpourings.

It is the organ of creation, the root of all things; the Deity produced it for the production of worlds.

The first modification of this most subtle substance was the creation of the elements.

The property of this universal fluid is purity and simplicity in the highest degree, as far as is possible for corporeal things.

<summary>This page defines the "universal fluid" (fluide universel) as the fundamental, divine substance of the universe that manifests as magnetism, electricity, and the classical elements. It establishes this fluid as the essential medium required to understand the phenomena of somnambulism.</summary>
<keywords>universal fluid, first matter, magnetism, electricity, elements, creation, natural philosophy, somnambulism</keywords>

Its property is action, inflow, and outflow.
Clothed in the most subtle body, it is called *Light*.

produces movement, activity, rotation, growth, and life.

The action of the effusions of this matter, and the action of matter upon these effusions, varies according to the properties of the bodies that contain these effusions within themselves.

In the finest bodies, such as in light, the movement of these effusions is exceedingly rapid.

The second degree, which follows *Light*, is the *Magnetic fluid*. <note>original: "Магнитическое жидкое вещество". This refers to the 18th-century concept of "fluidism," where magnetism and other forces were thought to be invisible, flowing substances.</note>

Being enclosed in particles that are not so fine, these effusions are not set into such subtle motion: and therefore they surround creation like a sea.

The third degree is the *Matter of heat*. <note>Likely a reference to "caloric," a historical scientific theory that heat was a fluid substance that flowed from hotter to colder bodies.</note> It consists of the compression of many physical particles, "pregnant" with outpourings of light; it therefore multiplies the internal action of this outpouring so that the effusions, by their nature, strive to return to their source; this striving of theirs constitutes the friction of particles, while the quiet dissolution of these particles produces heat.

The fourth degree is the *electrical force*.

<meta>This page continues the technical explanation of the "four degrees" of matter introduced on page 129, specifically detailing the mechanical and energetic relationship between "primary matter" and the physical particles that contain its emanations.</meta>

<page-num>130</page-num>

The effusions <note>original: "изліянія" (izliyaniya) — outpourings or emanations</note> of this primary matter are of such a nature that they constantly exert a backward action—as we have stated above—upon the very mass from which they emanate.

This reciprocal action corresponds to the quality of the bodies in which these effusions are contained, and to the resistance of the physical particles themselves.

If the resistance of these particles is not equal to the effusion—as is the case with light—then the primary matter acts with its full force, just as it does in light. If, however, the resistance and the force are equal to one another, so that neither the particles of the body overcome the force of the inflows <note>original: "втеченій" (vtecheniy) — inward flows or influxes</note>, nor the force of the inflows overcomes the force of the physical particles, then from this state of equality is born a fluid substance called *magnetic*.

It is like a pair of scales held in equilibrium, where the slightest imbalance causes one tray to tip.

If the striving <term>striving: "стремленіе" (stryemleniye) — in 18th-century natural philosophy, this refers to an inherent tendency, pressure, or conatus of a substance to move in a certain direction</term> of the struggling, enclosed forces of the outflows <note>original: "изтеченій" (iztecheniy) — outward flows</note> is stronger than the physical particles containing them, then the striving of these inflows to return to their source overcomes the resistance of the particles, so that they are gradually set free;

<summary>This page explains the mechanical balance between the "effusions" of primary matter and physical particles, defining magnetism as a state of perfect equilibrium between these forces. It describes how the tendency of these emanations to return to their source can eventually overcome the resistance of the matter containing them.</summary>

Let us explain this more clearly. In Nature there is no fire, but only light.

When light is enclosed in a body, by its nature it strives unceasingly to return to the primary matter from which it flows.

This effort consists in the fact that it tears apart the subtle particles of bodies; and this tearing, when it is not too strong, is heat.

Suns are not burning bodies, but bodies of light—worlds of a superior quality which, by their rapid rotation, set the atmospheric particles in motion, and through this motion produce heat, electric force, and fire.

Heat is nothing other than the effort of these <term>effusions: "изліянія" (izliyaniya) — outpourings or emanations from the source of light</term> to return to the primary matter and the tearing of the body's particles that enclose them. These particles, being carried along for some time by the stream of light, reveal themselves in a way that we can perceive; and this sensation we call heat.

One who observes the workings of Nature seeks help in times of sickness from humble herbs. Unspoiled instinct teaches him the direct use of these plants, and from them, he receives restoration and recovery.

The health of the body consists in the balance of the humors <note>In historical medicine, "humors" (or fluids) were the essential liquids within the body—traditionally blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—the balance of which was believed to determine a person's physical and mental health.</note>; an excess, or a deficiency, is the source of all diseases.

All such diverse diseases are merely the various consequences of a few underlying causes. The science of medicine lost its value when it began to treat the consequences without understanding the causes.

Hippocrates <note>Hippocrates of Kos (c. 460 – c. 370 BC), an ancient Greek physician often called the "Father of Medicine," emphasized clinical observation and the belief that the body has the ability to heal itself through natural means.</note> appeared and wrote of this for the benefit of humanity; but his excellent rules were soon forgotten, and madness and arrogance filled the cities with charlatans <note>The original Russian phrase "площадными лекарями" literally means "marketplace healers," referring to unlicensed or fraudulent practitioners who peddled cures in public squares.</note>. It refers to the moisture that leaves the body through the skin and lungs without the person noticing.</note> is the first which is subject to various accidents: sometimes it is slowed, and sometimes it is cut off entirely.

The moisture that should evaporate thickens, and the sweat-pores close; this thickened moisture, communicating with the bile and other humors <note>original: "сокамъ" (juices/humors). In the medical theory of the time, health depended on the fluidity and balance of these bodily liquids.</note>, thickens them as well. Chills are a deficiency of electrical force, while fever is an excess of it.

The truth of this conclusion is demonstrated by experiments. If a patient is positively electrified during a chill, the chill will pass entirely; if they are negatively electrified during a fever <term>fever</term> <note>original: "жаръ" (heat). In this medical context, the author uses "heat" to describe the symptomatic state of a fever.</note>, the fever will subside completely. This proves that medical art restores the disrupted equilibrium.

Excess in eating and drinking, as well as in physical exercise, is the source of many illnesses. Sometimes the blood thickens too much, and at other times it becomes too thin. The very nature of one’s diet disrupts the equilibrium of our humors <term>humors</term> <note>original: "сокахъ" (juices). In pre-modern medicine, these were the vital fluids (like blood, phlegm, and bile) that were believed to govern health and temperament.</note>, and frequently leads to disorders and disease.

The air we breathe is a source of both health and sickness. It possesses various properties, much like water. Swift streams flowing over a stony bed are the healthiest for drinking; however, those emerging from mountains—saturated with sulfur, vitriol <term>vitriol</term> <note>original: "купоросомъ". The causes of all infectious diseases are found in the air; and contagion acts upon humans, more or less, according to the quality of their humors <term>humors</term> <note>original: "соки" (juices). Smoke from firewood is a certain kind of gas—by this name I shall call every evaporation issuing from a body into the air;—it carries with it inflammable air <term>inflammable air</term> <note>original: "горючій воздухъ" (goryuchiy vozdukh). Smoke from coals and evaporations from spoiled plants and rotting animals are also harmful.

### ESSENTIAL RULES FOR THE KNOWLEDGE OF ELECTRICAL AND MAGNETIC FORCE.

1. Electrical force is of two kinds: aerial and terrestrial.

2. Atmospheric force originates two or three hundred paces from the earth.

3. At noon, in calm weather and under a clear sky, atmospheric electrical force is *positive*; but in the morning and evening, it is *negative*.

4. Water vapors repel it, while dew and fog attract it.

5. Atmospheric electrical force has a great influence on all bodies, especially on organized beings <note>The author uses "organized beings" to refer to living organisms—plants, animals, and humans—which possess a structured biological system.</note>.

6. Organized beings are the best *electrometers* <term>electrometers</term> <note>Instruments used to measure electrical charge. The author argues that living bodies are more sensitive to electricity than any mechanical device of the time.</note>, or measurers of this force.

### ON THE MEDICAL EFFECTS OF ELECTRICAL FORCE.

Chevalier <note>original: "Кавалеръ" (Kavaler). A title of nobility, likely corresponding to the French 'Chevalier'.</note> Soucelier de la Tour <note>Likely referring to a scientist or physician from the late 18th century; during this period, many "gentleman scientists" experimented with "medical electricity."</note> loved to practice Medicine, believing that the science which brings healing and relief to man is the most important and useful of all. Being compassionate toward poverty and misfortune, he never missed an opportunity where his knowledge could be useful to people. Seeing, however, that the medicines he prescribed did not always produce the expected effect, he resolved to follow a different path. Noticing that sharp objects have the property of drawing away the <term>electrical matter</term> <note>At the time, electricity was theorized to be a physical fluid or "matter" that could be moved or drained from the body.</note> to another place, he hit upon the idea of seeking in this method what he had sought in vain in herbs and roots.

He fashioned for himself two small discs out of cork-wood, and into each he inserted

Pain in a hollow, decayed tooth, he says, is not caused by the nerve being exposed and the outside air entering there, but by <term>thickened humors</term> <note>The original Russian "мокротъ" (mokrot) refers to the medical theory of "humors"—bodily fluids that were believed to cause pain and disease when they became stagnant or congested.</note>; for, once the illness is cured, the tooth and the nerve remain in the same condition.

He electrified patients in the following way:—He placed the patient on an <term>insulator</term> <note>A platform or stool, usually with glass legs, designed to prevent the electrical charge from grounding through the floor.</note>, connected them to the conductor, and held his small disk fitted with needles—from which the <term>electrical matter</term> <note>At this time, electricity was conceptualized as a physical "fluid" or "matter" that could flow like water.</note> flowed—against the aching cheek at a distance of six <term>lines</term> <note>An old unit of measurement; six lines is roughly equivalent to half an inch (12.7 mm).</note>, directly opposite the tooth. After turning the globe sixty times, which took about a minute, he would stop the electrification; frequently, the patient felt no more pain after this.

Many who suffered from a <term>tertian fever</term> <note>A form of malaria characterized by a fever that returns every third day.</note> were cured by him in three days, by making 60 to 80 rotations of the globe, depending on the age and physical constitution of the patient.

The best time for electrification is on non-feverish days; three days are sufficient, and there is no need to be electrified more than twice.

He cured a 36-year-old woman who had suffered for two years from constipation, for which no medications had provided her any relief. Суселье" refers to the French practitioner Sousselier de la Tour, whose electrical experiments were being shared across Europe.</note> electrified her every morning using the same method, but to no avail. ### SOME NEW EXTRAORDINARY EXPERIMENTS

I have made for myself a special kind of <term>amalgam</term> <note>A mixture or alloy of mercury with another metal, used here to increase the friction and conductivity of the electrical machine.</note> from zinc and mercury.

Taking one part zinc and three parts mercury, I combined them in the known manner and added as much <term>white lead</term> <note>Historically, a white pigment made of lead carbonate; here it is used to give the mixture the necessary thick consistency.</note> as was necessary to make a thick mixture, into which I poured melted sheep tallow, forming a type of ointment. Whenever I set about the task of electrifying, I would smear the cushions <note>Leather friction pads that rub against the rotating glass to generate static electricity.</note> near the <term>glass sphere</term> <note>The central component of an 18th-century electrostatic generator, which was rotated rapidly against the cushions to produce a charge.</note> with this ointment; and the thinner it was applied, the better the effect. The action was very powerful. Having turned the sphere a few times, I obtained an extraordinarily large spark; and this experiment can be performed in any weather. <note>High humidity often prevented electrical experiments from working; the author is noting that this specific grease protects the machine from moisture.</note>

<page-num>162</page-num>

Equally-charged bodies <note>original: "Равноэлектричныя тѣла" — bodies possessing the same amount or type of electrical fluid.</note> do not act upon one another; rather, one must possess more of this force than the other.

One may increase or decrease the electrical force through <term>artifice</term> <note>original: "искусствомъ" — referring here to the skilled application of magnetic or electric techniques by the practitioner.</note>: the former by *positive* and the latter by *negative* electrification.

Positive and negative bodies exist in a state of full electrical interaction with one another, provided that the proportion between these bodies is correctly balanced.

Two bodies existing in a state of full electrical interaction are, in regard to their electrical connection, so to speak, a single body in two parts. <note>This is a central tenet of Mesmerism: the idea that the "magnetizer" and the "subject" become a single functional organism linked by an invisible fluid.</note>

When two bodies are brought into full electrical interaction, no change can occur in one without a corresponding change following in the other.

Since a change in one body exerts an influence on the change in the other, then a change in the <term>sensations</term> <note>original: "ощущеній" — encompassing both physical feelings and internal perceptions.</note> of one body must necessarily influence the sensations of the other; for sensations are nothing other than a <term>change of state</term> <note>original: "перемѣна состоянія" — the author argues that because "electricity" links the two bodies, any physical or mental "state change" is instantly transmitted through the shared fluid.</note>.

### Animal Magnetism.

That fluid substance which <term>Mesmer</term> <note>Franz Anton Mesmer (1734–1815), a German physician who believed an invisible natural force, "animal magnetism," flowed through all living things and could be manipulated for healing.</note> called *Animal Magnetism* is nothing other than the second degree of light in relation to the physical world, as we have stated above.

It is an extremely subtle <term>essence</term> <note>original: "существо" — while this can mean "a being," in 18th-century Russian scientific and mystical contexts, it often refers to a "substance" or "quintessence."</note>, elastic, and capable of penetrating every body.

As it is the lightest of all fluid substances, it restores equilibrium in all things and prevents their mixing, from which <term>Chaos</term> <note>Refers to the primordial state of the universe before the divine order was established.</note> would once again arise.

This fluid substance, the child of light, possesses all its mobility and is the cause of the motion of all things.

<summary>The author describes the spiritual "mystery of unity" before defining Animal Magnetism as a subtle, light-like fluid that maintains the order of the universe and prevents it from collapsing back into chaos.</summary>
<keywords>Animal Magnetism, Mesmer, unity, Nature, Deity, equilibrium, chaos, subtle fluid, child of light</keywords>

By its power, the <term>juices</term> <note>original: "соки" — In 18th-century medical terms, this referred to vital bodily fluids like blood, lymph, and humors.</note> move within the body of an animal.

Mineral magnetism, combustibility, or <term>phlogiston</term> <note>An obsolete scientific theory that a fire-like element called "phlogiston" was contained within combustible bodies and released during burning.</note>, and all other such phenomena are its effects.

Here, the author suggests electricity is a "heavier" or "clothed" version of the magnetic fluid.</note>, until it is freed once again from these adopted parts and returns in purity to its source.

Electrical matter is the first modification of the magnetic fluid,

<meta>This page concludes the author's explanation of the hierarchy of natural forces, specifically refining the relationship between electricity and magnetism as "degrees of light." <page-num>166</page-num>

...and the third degree of light (*). The soul of the **electrical substance** is the **magnetic fluid**: electrical matter is the same substance, only contained within a coarser shell.

Proof of this is that the magnetic force passes even through glass, whereas the electrical force does not pass through it. <note>In 18th-century physics, it was observed that a magnet could attract iron through a glass pane, while the "electrical fluid" (static electricity) was blocked or insulated by glass, leading many to believe magnetism was a more "subtle" or "pure" form of the same energy.</note>

The purer the air—the more it is cleansed of <term>combustibility</term> <note>original: "горючесть" (combustibility). In this context, it refers to "phlogiston," a hypothetical element formerly believed to be contained within combustible bodies and released during burning.</note>—the closer it is to magnetic matter.

The more combustibility there is in the air, the more prone it is to ignition, and the closer it is to **electrical matter**.

---

(*) Previously, the Author called the electrical force the fourth degree, yet here he calls it the third. From this, we observe only (as is evident from all his writings) the fecundity of the Author and the abundance of his knowledge; because of this, he did not "compose," so to speak, but only wrote, merely pouring his thoughts onto paper, and perhaps sometimes did not find the time to reread what he had written. Otherwise, he would certainly have noticed this contradiction himself, given how close these passages stand to one another. It occurred only because he has now lost sight of the "matter of heat," which he had included previously. For this reason, we have decided in our translation to leave both these passages without our corrections, in their innocent negligence. <note>This footnote was written by the original Russian translator or editor of the manuscript, providing a rare glimpse into their view of the author as a divinely inspired, if somewhat disorganized, genius.</note>

<summary>The author defines electricity as a "coarser" form of the universal magnetic fluid and explains that pure air facilitates magnetism while air rich in "combustibility" (phlogiston) facilitates electricity. An editorial note explains a contradiction in the author's numbering of these forces as a result of his rapid, unedited writing style.</summary>

and their sensations are the same; and this state is called *harmony*. <note>In the context of 18th-century vitalism, "harmony" is more than an aesthetic term; it describes a state of health where the body’s internal vibrations are perfectly synchronized with the universe.</note>

The measure of gradual tension is the cause of harmony in music: likewise in the <term>living being</term> <note>The original Russian "животномъ" refers to the "animal" kingdom or any organism possessing an *anima* (life force), including humans.</note>.

There are correct chords, concord, and discord upon the instrument of our nerves. <note>This imagery treats the nervous system like a stringed instrument, such as a harpsichord, that can be tuned to health or fall into the "discord" of disease.</note>

A person absorbs through the pores magnetic matter, which, according to the external structure of the nerves—its conductors—flows from the head into the other external parts of the body.

A tree receives this matter through the roots, bark, and leaves.

The transfer of magnetic matter from one body to another is called *magnetism*; while the techniques that teach how to transfer this matter from one body to another are called *magnetizing*. <note>This is a foundational definition of "Animal Magnetism" or Mesmerism, a precursor to modern hypnotism and biofield therapies.</note>

### ON MAGNETIZING.

A person who wishes to magnetize another must have the power to multiply the magnetic matter within them and to facilitate its <insert>circulation</insert> within them... <note>The original text cuts off at "обраще-" (circulation), likely continuing on the following page.</note>

<summary>This page defines harmony as the synchronization of nervous sensations and explains how "magnetic matter" flows through humans and plants. It introduces the practice of "magnetizing" as the intentional transfer of this vital fluid between individuals.</summary>

<keywords>harmony, magnetic matter, nerves, animal magnetism, Mesmerism, vital fluid, physiology</keywords>

--- Page 189 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 168 regarding the requirements for a magnetizer, specifically finishing the sentence about the internal circulation of magnetic matter.</meta>

<page-num>169</page-num>

...circulation, without disturbing the balance of fluids <note>The original Russian "соковъ" literally translates as "juices," a reference to the "humors" of the body—a concept from ancient medicine still prevalent in the 18th and early 19th centuries.</note> within their own body.

Every healthy person, filled with magnetic matter, is capable of acting upon one who is weaker.

The common method of magnetizing is as follows:

The magnetizer sits opposite the person they wish to magnetize, face to face; and in order to bring themselves into *harmony* with them, they place their hands upon the other’s shoulders and draw them down from the shoulders to the very tips of the fingers, touching the thumb of each of the patient’s hands for some time. This is repeated two or three times; afterward, the magnetizing begins from head to toe. Here, through touch <note>The Russian "осязаніе" refers to the sense of touch or physical palpation used to "feel" for magnetic blockages.</note> or questioning, one attempts to discover the location of the illness, which for the most part is found on the opposite side; especially in cases of paralysis, rheumatism, and other similar diseases. Once this location is found, they move their hands over it until the illness reaches a *crisis* <note>In the practice of Animal Magnetism, a "crisis" was a visible physical reaction—such as a tremor, convulsion, or deep trance—thought to be the necessary turning point where the body expelled the disease.</note>, which is the sole means to a complete cure.

Instead of the bare hand, a blunt-ended rod made of iron or some other metal may also be used; however, a **glass rod** is best of all, ten to fifteen inches in length and three to six <term>lines</term> <note>A "line" (*lineya*) was a traditional unit of measurement roughly equal to 1/10th of an inch or 2.54 millimeters.</note> thick, tapering at the upper end to one or two lines. The rod is more effective if it is rubbed with a magnet, but in cases of eye diseases, cramps, and similar conditions, this is harmful.

It should be noted that when magnetizing with tools rather than just the hands, one must switch the **poles**. That is to say: with an iron rod or a glass rod in the right hand, one must move along the patient's right side, and with the left hand, along the left. What magnetizers call "placing pole against pole" involves touching the head, chest, abdomen, etc., with the right hand while holding the left hand behind the back of the head—specifically behind the spine, where a multitude of **nerves** travel toward the stomach.

The **meridian** is the name for the perpendicular line beginning from the middle of the forehead which divides the person in two; the patient’s right side is called the North— <note>The text ends mid-word with the catchword "Nor-," likely leading into "North" or "Northern" on the following page, continuing the analogy of the human body as a compass or planetary body with magnetic poles.</note>

To treat diseases with the help of **trees**, they must be magnetized in the following manner:

Choose a young, strong, branchy, straight tree without dead knots, especially a thick oak, elm, or beech; and standing opposite it from the southern side, begin to move your finger, or an iron rod, or a small stick, from the leaves toward the branches, having first drawn several such lines along the main branch. Then move downward to the roots, repeating this several times and using your left hand for the right side of the tree, and your right hand for the left side. After this, magnetize the northern side of the tree as well; then the roots themselves, and finally embrace the whole tree several times along all its **poles** <note>In Mesmerist theory, all natural objects were believed to have magnetic poles, similar to the Earth, which could be balanced or manipulated for health.</note>.

Healthy people, after holding onto this tree for a long time, feel its effect, while the sick experience a **crisis** <term>crisis</term> <note>Original: *кризисъ*. To treat many people at once, ropes are tied to the top of the tree and to the main branch—

...etc. However, Mr. Mesmer, in cases of **putrid fever** <note>original: "гнилая горячка" — a historical medical term used to describe typhus or other severe infectious fevers accompanied by exhaustion.</note> and when there was spoiled mucus in the stomach, would administer emetics and laxatives. The Bremen Doctor, Mr. Bicker, sent to Mr. Baldinger the following letter concerning *animal magnetism*, which was published in the *Hanover Magazine*, in the nineteenth book of 1787, and in *Baldinger’s Medical Journal* in the twelfth book, page 76.

> "A short time ago, I had the honor of informing you of a new method of treatment called *animal magnetism*, whether such a name has been given to it correctly or incorrectly. I do not think it necessary to explain the difference between Mr. Mesmer's method, which is already well known, and our own; rather, I shall endeavor only to present the method we employ as clearly and understandably as possible."

<meta>This page continues the letter from Dr. Bicker (begun on page 178) describing the practical preparations and procedures used in the administration of animal magnetism, specifically the preparation of "magnetized water."</meta>

<page-num>179</page-num>

> “For a week before the actual **treatment** <term>the formal application of animal magnetism to the patient</term>, patients drank a bottle of **magnetized water** <term>water believed to be infused with 'vital fluid' or 'animal magnetism' through specific manual gestures</term> daily, which acted as a mild laxative for them. The water is magnetized in the following manner. —
>
> “Take a clean, empty glass with one hand, placing all five fingers spread wide against the bottom; with the other hand, also with fingers spread, grasp the top edge. For several minutes, rotate the glass with the lower fingers toward your body in a consistent direction. After this, take two bottles—one filled with water and one empty. Pour the water into that glass, holding it firmly by the base as described above, and with the fingers of the other hand pressed together, make a motion for several minutes as if you were **throwing something into the water**; finally, pour it through a funnel into the empty bottle.
>
> “This procedure is followed every day that the water is given to the patient to drink. Call this **superstition** <note>original: "предразсудкомъ" — can also mean prejudice or a preconceived notion</note>, or **imagination** <note>the role of 'imagination' was a central point of debate for skeptics who believed the effects of Mesmerism were purely psychological</note>, or whatever you wish: yet through various experiments, we have found that patients could always distinguish magnetized water from any other.”

However simple this method is, and however ridiculous it may seem to many: nevertheless, experience shows it is so, and this confirms the proposition: that causes which appear minor on the surface often produce significant effects.

A remarkable <term>crisis</term> <note>original: "кризисъ" (krizis). In the context of 18th-century "animal magnetism," a crisis was a physical or psychological reaction—such as a trance or convulsions—believed to be a necessary peak in the healing process.</note>, called *clairvoyant sleep* <note>original: "прозорливый сонъ" (prozorlivyy son). A state of heightened intuition or foresight attributed to the magnetic trance.</note>, or *magnetic somnambulism*, occurred regularly in one female patient every day and lasted from 3 to 5 hours; sometimes an entire day; rarely two or three days: while the longest sleep lasted five days and nights. During this long sleep, the female patients were always cheerful and alert during the day, but at night were in some kind of dark sensations: but they were never in a state of complete oblivion. Meanwhile, natural sleep would also come to them for several hours, from which they would pass back into that same [magnetic] state, being unable to distinguish or explain this transition. Despite this magnetic sleep, they were still magnetized daily, both because they themselves <note>The sentence breaks off here, likely continuing with the patient's own requests for treatment on the following page.</note>

They felt that the very act of waking proved the truth of the phenomenon and confirmed its authenticity beyond doubt. The patient, who had previously spoken to them with such simplicity, freedom, and lack of restraint, fell into a natural shyness, confusion, and astonishment upon waking when she saw people entirely unknown to her standing by her bed.

Upon their recovery, we noticed a significant decrease in the sharpness of their sensory organs, especially the sense of touch. <note>In the "magnetic" state, patients were often reported to have hyper-acute senses, sometimes claiming to "see" with their skin or fingertips. This is the main information that I can communicate to you about this new method of treatment, partly from my own experiments and partly from the observations of my colleagues. Although I am certain that people will laugh at this, speak ill of it, and dispute everything: but in Medicine, experience is more important than all

The direct path to truth and certainty is, without doubt, rational and constant observation and testing; and where is it more necessary than in this case? Usually, at this point, people cry out for "reason." They say that whatever cannot be proven by reason is all untrue: and yet...

It would be strange, given the known limitations of our knowledge and concepts, to deny and proclaim something as madness solely because it is incomprehensible to us; and it is even worse to reject it out of pride, envy, or stubbornness. <term>Socrates</term>, the wisest of all philosophers, said at the time of his death: “I have seen much in <term>Nature</term> <note>original: "Натурѣ" (Nature); here referring to the natural order and the physical world.</note>, I have tested it, and I have found that it is good: therefore, I do not doubt that even that which I have not yet seen or tested is also good.”

<meta>This page continues Dr. Bicker’s transition from a philosophical defense of magnetism to a historical genealogy of the practice. ### REMARKS ON ANIMAL MAGNETISM.

Traces of <term>animal magnetism</term> <note>original: "магнитизмѣ животныхъ" (animal magnetism). A theory popularized by Franz Mesmer suggesting an invisible natural force possessed by all living beings that could have healing effects.</note> are found even in deepest antiquity, in India.

The priestly class of <term>Brahmins</term> <note>The highest Hindu caste, traditionally responsible for religious rituals and preserving sacred texts.</note> in Hindustan for several centuries practiced this science, which derived its origin and rules from the Divine Service established by <term>Brahma</term>, and from oral traditions.

In the <term>Vedas</term> <note>original: "Воданѣ" (Vodane). This is likely an archaic or corrupted spelling of the Vedas, the oldest scriptures of Hinduism.</note> of Brahma we find confirmation of this, and the teachings of <term>Zoroaster</term> <note>An ancient Persian prophet who founded Zoroastrianism.</note> and the <term>Zend-Avesta</term> <note>The primary collection of religious texts of Zoroastrianism.</note> allow us to conclude with certainty that the Persians in Asia, and through them the Egyptian priests in their mysteries, borrowed from this source the concept of magnetism and its miraculous effects.

<term>Baldinger</term> <note>Ernst Gottfried Baldinger (1738–1804), a prominent German physician and editor of a widely read medical journal.</note>, in his *Medical Journal* of 1787, in the thirteenth volume on page 26, writes the following about magnetism:

The <term>Jesuits</term>, at the very beginning of their order, brought to Europe the practical teaching of these mysteries from India...

<summary>Dr. Bicker traces the historical roots of animal magnetism back to ancient India, specifically the Brahmins and the Vedas, suggesting it spread to Persia and Egypt before being brought to Europe by Jesuit missionaries.</summary>

<keywords>animal magnetism, history of medicine, Brahmins, Brahma, Zoroaster, Zend-Avesta, Ernst Baldinger, Jesuits, India, Persia</keywords>

--- Page 215 ---
<meta>This page continues Dr. Bicker’s historical account of magnetism, specifically focusing on the role of the Jesuit Order in preserving and transmitting these "secret sciences" from the East to Europe. It addresses contemporary criticisms of the Jesuits and highlights their influence on Franz Mesmer.</meta>

<page-num>195</page-num>

...through their missionaries. It is well known that in no other monastic order are there men as learned as theirs; and the immortal works of **Kircher** <note>Athanasius Kircher (1602–1680), a Jesuit polymath who published some of the earliest extensive studies on magnetism, viewing it as a universal force of attraction.</note> and **Caspar Schott** <note>A student of Kircher and a Jesuit scientist whose works on "natural magic" documented various physical phenomena and mechanical inventions.</note> bear witness to how long they have been skilled in the <term>secret sciences</term> <note>In this context, the "secret" or "occult" sciences refer to natural phenomena—like magnetism or electricity—that were not yet fully understood by mainstream science and were often associated with ancient traditions.</note>. **Mesmer** himself, who was presented as the inventor of magnetism, owes the very foundation of his knowledge to the famous **Father Hell** <note>Maximilian Hell (1720–1792), a Jesuit astronomer in Vienna. In 1774, he used shaped steel magnets to treat patients, a practice that directly inspired Mesmer's early work before Mesmer transitioned to "animal" (non-mineral) magnetism.</note>, who was a most learned man.

It seems to me quite unjust that the author of the letter published in **Baldinger’s journal** <note>Referring back to Ernst Baldinger’s *Medical Journal* mentioned on the previous page.</note> judges so harshly, accusing the members of this society <note>The Society of Jesus, or the Jesuits.</note> decisively and without evidence, as if they employed these sciences only for the secret designs of their order.

If one looks at the other works of **Kircher**, **Schott**, and many other such famous **Jesuits**, you will see that these men had no intention of concealing even the most secret matters, provided they could be of benefit to humanity.

**Schott**, in the fourth volume of his *Medical Magic* <note>original: "врачебной Магіи". Likely referring to a section of Schott’s *Physica Curiosa* or *Magia Universalis*, where he discusses the wonders of nature and medicine.</note>, writes of secret remedies and discusses the effect of the voice and of musical rhythm <note>original: "музыкальномъ тактѣ" (musical tact/measure). This refers to the theory that rhythmic vibrations and sound could influence the human body's internal "fluids" or "ethers."</note>.

<summary>Bicker defends the Jesuit Order against claims of secrecy, arguing that their scholars like Kircher and Schott were the true pioneers of magnetic science and that Franz Mesmer's work was fundamentally based on the teachings of the Jesuit Father Hell.</summary>

<keywords>Jesuits, Athanasius Kircher, Caspar Schott, Father Hell, Mesmer, secret sciences, Medical Magic, Baldinger's Journal, history of magnetism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Dr. Bicker’s defense of the Jesuit scholars, specifically Athanasius Kircher, and transitions into a philosophical discussion on why "secret sciences" were historically kept from the general public to prevent their misuse.</meta>

<page-num>196</page-num>

**Kircher** specifically speaks of **animal magnetism** <note>A term later popularized by Mesmer, but here Bicker notes it was discussed by Kircher a century earlier to describe the subtle connections between living organisms.</note>, of medical magnetism, of musical magnetism, and of the magnetism of the passions. Within these books is hidden a great and deep knowledge of <term>Nature</term> <note>In the 18th century, "Nature" was often personified or viewed as a complex, divinely ordered system whose laws were being uncovered by "natural philosophers."</note>. And for that reason, in good conscience, I cannot approve of the fact that these men, who have rendered so many services to the learned world, are not given their due justice and are deprived of the honor of their inventions.

In my opinion, the treasures of the <term>mysteries</term> <note>original: "таинства". This refers to esoteric knowledge or "arcana" rather than religious sacraments.</note> were in better hands then than they are now. I do not know if it is useful to reveal the <term>secret sciences</term> to everyone; we see that even the best things, when they fall into the hands of the ill-intentioned and the imprudent, are used for evil; and I do not condemn the manner of writing used by the ancients, who hid the **seed within the shell** <note>A common metaphor for esoteric or allegorical writing, where the core truth (the seed) is protected by a difficult outer layer (the shell) to ensure only the worthy can access it.</note>. He who reflects and is capable of understanding will know how to crack the shell and distinguish it from the seed; but for the common crowd, the food of great minds is not suitable.

And so the accusation against these people—as if they used the <term>knowledge</term> of hidden things to deceive the public—is completely...

<summary>Bicker praises Athanasius Kircher's early theories on various forms of magnetism and argues that "secret sciences" were rightfully kept from the public in the past to prevent misuse by the "ill-intentioned."</summary>

->Most Reverend Father in Christ.<-

On the day of the <term>Assumption of the Most Holy Mother of God</term> <note>original: "Успенія Пресвятыя Богоматери"; a major Christian feast day celebrated on August 15th. It seemed as if the Most Holy Virgin herself, in the masses of water risen from the sea, wished to present a picture of paradise, where, turning one’s mind and eyes, one could behold as in a mirror everything that one could possibly desire; and, having been an eyewitness to it, I can call it the mirror of all mirrors. The sea off <term>Sicily</term> <note>An island off the southern coast of Italy.</note> swelled and rose for about ten miles in length, in the form of a large black sandy mountain, and the <term>Calabrian</term> <note>Referring to Calabria, the "toe" of the Italian peninsula, located across the strait from Sicily.</note> plain in the blink of an eye turned into pure transparent glass, like a mirror, and with its upper edge it joined the mountain of water, and with its lower edge it joined Calabria. In this mirror there immediately appeared a row of whitish columns, numbering more than ten thousand, of the same measure and height, and at an equal distance from one another. But suddenly all these columns disappeared and turned into canals, or <term>aqueducts</term> <note>original: "акведюки"; historical structures used to transport water, usually characterized by long rows of arches.</note>, such as there are...

This is the wondrous phenomenon which for twenty years I did not believe, regarding it as a fairy tale, but which I now find to be true, and far more beautiful than I could have imagined.    From this letter, it is clear that Father Angelucci saw this phenomenon not in the air, but in the water—or more accurately, on the surface of the water. Schott <note>original: "Шоштъ"; likely a misspelling of Caspar Schott (1608–1666), a Jesuit scientist and Kircher’s most famous student.</note> posits that such a magnificent arrangement, with such diversity and brilliance of colors, could not be seen in the air, which is constantly in motion. On the contrary, there is much more possibility of seeing it in the water, which, having expanded, gradually rises toward **Sicily**, and being stirred into various forms by a gentle movement of the air, it takes on the appearance of a polygonal mirror, suited for many different reflections.

   Kircher <note>original: "Кирхеръ"; Athanasius Kircher, a renowned 17th-century polymath who studied the Strait of Messina.</note> attributes this to the following causes. He noted that the mountain standing opposite **Reggio** casts a long, dark shadow from **Calabria** toward Peloro <note>original: "Пелору"; Capo Peloro, the northeastern tip of Sicily forming the narrowest part of the Strait of Messina.</note>. <meta>Continuing the explanation of Athanasius Kircher regarding the physical causes of the Fata Morgana, specifically how atmospheric particles create a "polygonal mirror" effect, and transitioning into historical accounts of similar aerial phenomena.</meta>

<page-num>202</page-num>

...heat <note>Completing the sentence from the previous page: "This sand, from the strong sun's [heat]..."</note> lifted with vapors to a height, creates various planes in the air. These, being shaded by the shadows cast from the mountains, form a **polygonal mirror**. Within this mirror, depending on the point of observation, various objects are reflected. Because of the sheer multitude of these mirrors, a single standing soldier may appear as an entire army when the light rays reflect his image into the clouds. The same occurs with trees and animals. There is no doubt that the sun truly lifts earthy, glassy, and other particles upward; for we often find hair, straw, sand, wood chips, and all sorts of debris trapped inside hail.

   Similar phenomena are mentioned by Damascius <note>a 6th-century Neoplatonist philosopher</note> in the *Life of Isidore* as recorded by Photius <note>a 9th-century scholar and Patriarch of Constantinople who compiled summaries of many lost ancient works</note>. He speaks of soldiers seen fighting in the air near Sicily on the so-called **Field of Four Towers** <note>original: "Четыребашенномъ полѣ"</note> and in many other places; this occurs for the most part during the summer heat and at midday.

   It is said that similar phenomena are seen in the city of Nereto in Apulia, as can be read in Scipione Mazzella’s <note>original: "Сципіона Мацеллюса"; an Italian historian who published a famous description of the Kingdom of Naples in 1586</note> description of Naples. Likewise, Cor- <note>The text cuts off, likely referring to Cornelius Agrippa, a famous 16th-century polymath and occultist known for discussing natural wonders.</note>

# I and THOU,
### A REMARKABLE CHAPTER, BELONGING TO THE PRECEDING ONE.

In Nature there is so much that is great, so much that is incomprehensible to man! And yet the solution to these riddles is closer to us than we think.
   I and thou — these are important words, providing many solutions to obscure concepts.
   What is *I* without *thou*? — A being completely separate, without connection. Nature did not create solitary beings: her law is community <note>original: "сообщество"; implying fellowship or a collective state</note>.
   The works of God consist in union: unhappy is that link which separates itself from the universal chain.
   In Nature everything is connected one to another, and this necessary connection constitutes the laws for the preservation of the material <note>original: "тѣлеснаго"; literally "corporeal" or "physical"</note> world.

<meta>Continuing the philosophical discussion on "I and Thou" from page 204, focusing on the interdependence of all levels of existence from nature to angels.</meta>

To every being, the limits of its activity <note>original: "дѣятельности"; refers to the sphere of influence or purpose a creature has in the world</note> are shown. Without a connection with other beings, there is no progress, no perfection, no activity.

Thus the plant depends on the earth that feeds it; the sky generously pours its dew into the earth's depths <note>original: "нѣдра"; literally "womb" or "inner depths," suggesting a nurturing relationship between sky and soil</note>, and she communicates these heavenly gifts to the plants and trees.

Everything is good as long as this mutual assistance and interaction continues: but when it ceases, then disorder begins.

If the sky were to become stingy with its dew, and the earth were to lock away the treasures it received from the air within its depths; if there were no mutual cooperation <note>original: "содѣйствованія"; implying a working-together of various forces</note>: then there would be no life—only death.

Thus: this mutual striving toward beneficence <note>original: "благотворенію"; the act of doing good or showing kindness</note> for one another is necessary, from the grain of sand to the stone, from the blade of grass to the beast, from the beast to man, from man to the Angel: This cooperation gradually becomes more noble by the hour, and its powers ever more majestic.

# On Longevity and the Art of Rejuvenation

If movement stops in any part of our body, then that part dies and is no longer capable of any action.

If, however, movement stops throughout the entire body, then it is often completely impossible to restore it, and the whole body then dies.

Our bodily life, says Wenzel <note>Likely a reference to Gottfried Wenzel (1762–1795), a popular author of the period who wrote on health, the prolongation of life, and "animal magnetism."</note>, is the product of humors <note>original: "влажностей" - literally 'moistures' or 'fluids,' referring to the ancient and early-modern medical theory that health depends on the balance of bodily fluids.</note>,

<term>Divine Wisdom of the Lord (Премудрость Господня)</term> — In this context, Wisdom is not just an abstract idea but a creative force that knows the inner workings of nature and science.
<term>The Wise Man (Мудрый)</term> — Not merely an intelligent person, but one who has been initiated into the deeper mysteries of God and nature.
<term>Longevity (долголѣтная жизнь)</term> — A major focus of late 18th-century "vitalist" philosophy, which sought ways to extend human life through natural and spiritual means.
<term>Rejuvenation (молодѣть)</term> — The art of regaining youthfulness, often linked in alchemical and Rosicrucian circles to the "Elixir of Life."
<term>Wenzel (Венцель)</term> — An authority cited by the author, representative of the era's fascination with health, diet, and the "vital force."

<meta>This page continues the discussion on longevity and physiology based on the writings of Wenzel. 223

...passing through all the appropriate vessels.

The forces by which these humors <note>original: "влажности" (moistures/fluids). In 18th-century medicine, "humors" were the vital fluids thought to govern health and temperament.</note> move act upon these vessels everywhere with such power that if the particles worn down by them were not renewed, their complete destruction would necessarily follow.

Consequently, the life of an animal <note>The term "animal" (животнаго) here is used in the broad 18th-century sense to mean any "animate" or living being, including humans, in their biological capacity.</note> is itself the instrument of death.

Lest the death of the animal occur prematurely, Nature <note>Capitalized in the original, personifying the natural world as a restorative force.</note> renews what is lost through food.

But this renewal occurs, first of all, through the work of the vessels in the animal's body, which digest food particles into substances appropriate for the animal's body.

For this chemical work <note>original: "химической работѣ." The use of "chemical" in physiology was an emerging concept in the late 1700s, reflecting the era's shift toward seeing the body as a laboratory of sorts.</note> of the vessels, an unimpeded circulation of humors is needed, a movement of them consistent with the solid particles, and finally, the softness of the vessels and their ability to expand or dilate. This equilibrium of juices <note>original: "соковъ" – another term for the bodily fluids or humors.</note> and the connection of parts constitute the health of man.

When this connection is broken, then disease begins; when it is in the bo- <note>The text breaks off mid-word ("тѣ-"), likely continuing as "тѣлѣ" (body) on the following page.</note>

<term>Vessels (сосуды)</term> — Refers to the veins, arteries, and other channels through which blood and "humors" flow.
<term>Chemical Work (химическая работа)</term> — The process of digestion and assimilation viewed through the lens of early modern science.
<term>Equilibrium (равновѣсіе)</term> — A central concept in vitalist medicine, where health is defined as a perfect balance between various physical and spiritual forces.
<term>Juices (соки)</term> — A synonym for humors or bodily fluids, often used to describe the essence extracted from food during digestion.

From Anatomy <note>The study of the structure of the body.</note> it is known that in old age the vessels ossify <note>original: "костенѣютъ" — to turn into bone or harden.</note>, and that bones are of a calcareous <note>original: "известковатаго" — lime-like or chalky.</note> composition: therefore, this ossification is the effect of flammable matter. All hot drinks mixed with a large quantity of flammable matter produce a calcareous sediment.

<term>Flammable Matter (горючее вещество)</term> — Known as "phlogiston," this was thought to be a physical substance that caused aging and disease when accumulated in the body through breathing.
<term>Ossification (окостенѣніе)</term> — The process by which soft tissues, like blood vessels, become hard and brittle like bone, which the author identifies as a primary sign of aging.

It must be noted here that throughout the entire journey, the peasant <note>Jacques Aymar</note> could not walk in any way other than ahead of Bossu, due to the nausea and heartache <note>original: "щемленія въ сердцѣ" (shchemleniya v serdtse), a physical sensation of constriction or sharp pain in the chest</note> which overcame him if he walked behind him. In the same manner, while at the site of the murder, he usually felt this heartache and a trembling in the body, though these sensations were not as strong when he was on the water.

As soon as the news of this case spread through Lyon, a multitude of opinions appeared both in confirmation and in refutation of it. Everyone judged according to their own notions, which were as crooked, as weak, and as unjust as is usually the case regarding extraordinary occurrences. Despite all the evidence, some still did not believe, and therefore they proceeded to a new experiment. They blindfolded him, so that he could not see the location, and so that he himself moved the rod <term>dowsing rod; a forked branch used to detect hidden substances or, in this case, traces of criminals</term> only when he wished; then, in the presence of witnesses, they led him into the cellar <note>the scene of the original crime</note>. However, the movement of the rod proved to be the same as it had been before.

### Opinions on this matter.

As soon as they became aware of the magic rod <term>dowsing rod; a forked branch used in this context to track criminals</term> and its extraordinary effects, they wished to explain the reasons for it. Some attributed this to magnetic force; others derived it from sympathy and antipathy <note>The 17th-century concept that natural objects possess innate attractions or repulsions toward one another</note>, and the followers of Aristotle found in it new confirmation of their doctrine of atoms. Neither were *real qualities*, *substantial forms*, nor *hidden properties* <note>original Latin: "qualitates reales," "formae substantiales," "virtutes occultae"</note> forgotten (*) and with these names they defended and refuted one and the same action.

Nature, in all its actions, possesses a single mechanism—a single way of manifesting its countless wonders—and it always proceeds by the simplest path, employs the easiest means, and does nothing without intention or in vain: everything within it has its purpose.

If only people never lost sight of the investigation of Nature,

---

<note>(*) Material qualities, essential forms, and hidden properties are terms of Aristotelian Philosophy.</note>

<summary>This page explores the various 17th-century scientific and philosophical theories—ranging from magnetism to Aristotelian "hidden properties"—proposed to explain how Jacques Aymar’s dowsing rod functioned. The author suggests that while scholars debate these terms, the answer lies in the simple, purposeful laws of Nature.</summary>

<keywords>natural philosophy, Aristotelianism, dowsing rod, magnetism, atoms, 17th-century science, occult properties, mechanism of nature</keywords>

then they certainly would not so often stray from the truth and fall into errors, which are often as harmful to the mind as they are to the heart.

Just as it is true that there is no effect without a cause—since nothing can produce itself—it is equally true that nothing can act upon an object without touching it. Everything depends on the manner of contact; herein lies the whole secret of the incomprehensible wonders of Nature <term>Nature; often personified in 17th and 18th-century thought as a rational, mechanical system of laws created by God</term>.

Often our spirit is too lazy for observation and investigation, and our senses are dull and slow; therefore, they cannot perceive the subtleties, speeds, and imperceptible forms by which bodies touch one another. To this is added human pride and scholastic arrogance <note>Refers to "schoolmen" or traditional academics of the era who were seen as prioritizing rigid dogma and abstract logic over empirical observation</note>, which rejects everything it does not know how to understand; meanwhile, ignorance and prejudice label all such things as the handiwork of hell.

Others have reached a crossroads only because of this false opinion: that Nature, in its hidden operations, follows entirely different paths, and as if to produce its wonders...

<meta>This page continues the argument from page 237, concluding the thought that Nature does not use supernatural means, but rather consistent physical laws that are sometimes invisible to the human eye.</meta>

238

...employs clever and extraordinary means, unlike those acting before our very eyes.

Nature acts always in the same way, and the entire wonder of her actions consists in the fact that the tools and means she employs do not fall under our senses, as they do in her ordinary actions, where with a single glance we can observe the cause, the action, and the consequence. For example: there is nothing wonderful <note>In this context, "wonderful" refers to the "miraculous" or "supernatural" rather than simply "pleasing."</note> in the fact that wood takes fire into itself and ignites from it, because Nature here hides nothing, and the entire process falls under our senses. When, on the contrary, dry, warm smoke—or vapor—rising from mines <note>The author likely refers to mineral veins or subterranean pits where sulfurous or acidic vapors were thought to escape and affect surface vegetation.</note> dries up and burns the plants and trees located on their surface, then this seems wonderful; only because these fine and sharp vapors are imperceptible to the senses. But is not the mechanism <term>mechanism; the philosophical view that the universe functions like a machine governed by physical laws of cause and effect</term> the same in both cases? —

This observation is worthy of mature reflection and offers a resolution to many of the mysteries of Nature.

<gloss>Nature, mechanism, vapors, wonder, mines</gloss>

<meta>This page builds on the previous discussion of invisible natural mechanisms by explaining that all physical interaction occurs through "vapors" or "atmospheres," which allow bodies to affect one another even when they do not appear to be touching.</meta>

239

Bodies touch one another by means of vapors emanating from them, or through an atmosphere surrounding the body. In this lies the secret of the dowsing rod <note>original: "волшебнаго прутика" (magic rod); the author refers to the practice of dowsing, where a forked branch is used to find water or minerals, here explained through physical emissions rather than magic.</note>.

These emanating vapors are either a part of the very essence of that from which they emerge, or a third substance that transfers force from the active part to the passive part <note>The original Russian "страждущую" literally means "suffering," but in the context of 18th-century natural philosophy, it refers to the "patient" or the object that receives an action from an "agent."</note>. Often, they are simply the air located near the active bodies; an example of this is a small bell, whose ringing travels to the eardrum of our ear. These emanating vapors, though they escape our sight, still retain the properties of the body from which they originated. We see this in damp weather, when water particles that have evaporated into the air settle again upon marble, walls, and so forth due to the cold, or fall as dew and rain, becoming water once more.

We observe the same with mercury <term>mercury; also known as quicksilver, it was a central subject of study because of its ability to evaporate and re-condense without losing its metallic properties</term>, which, when exposed to even the slightest heat, separates from all other bodies—no matter how thoroughly it was mixed with them—and appears again in its former state. The air receives into itself all manner of vapors, and wi—

...in which water is poured, and others containing another drink, for example wine and vodka. I place all three of these vessels upon an <term>insulator</term><note>original: изоляторъ (izolyator). If one were to slightly dampen a sponge and, hanging it by a thread over a vessel containing some liquid, set it to swing: then, by observing how long the sponge sticks to the vessel, one may also learn what is poured within it; it is only necessary to repeat several <term>experiments</term><note>original: опытъ (opyt). This term translates as both "experiment" and "experience," reflecting the era's view that wisdom comes from direct interaction with the physical world.</note>.

In everything there lies both truth and falsehood; and therefore, the rational person rejects nothing, but tests everything.

# Something about Witchcraft.

In former times, there were many such people who, in the language of the common folk, were called *Evil People*, *Wicked People*, or *Sorcerers*<note>original: Колдуны (Kolduny). In Russian folklore, a koldun was a male practitioner of magic, often viewed with a mix of fear and respect for his ability to both heal and harm.</note>, and who knew certain secret sciences, borrowed from the Arabs and Egyptians during the <term>Crusades</term><note>original: крестовые походы (krestovye pokhody). The author reflects a widespread Enlightenment-era view that occult or "secret" knowledge was brought to Europe from the East during the medieval holy wars.</note> and passed from one nation to another. Today, fortunately for humanity, these knowledges have almost been eradicated; however, in some places, especially in the villages, there are still people who do much harm with them, as I myself have happened to see.

The principal witchcraft usually consists of the following:

1. That the livestock loses its <term>cud</term><note>original: жвачка (zhvachka). Ruminants like cows and sheep must "chew the cud" to digest food; a cow "losing its cud" is a sign of serious digestive illness, which was historically attributed to a curse.</note>, eats nothing, and dies from it;
2. That its urine becomes bloody;
3. That a cow, instead of milk, gives blood when milked;
4. That lice attack the livestock;
5. That the chickens' heads and necks start to twist;
6. That a horse suddenly goes lame;
7. That the butter will not <term>churn</term><note>original: пахтаться (pakhtat'sya). The process of separating butter from milk. Failure to produce butter from cream was one of the most common domestic mishaps blamed on local witches.</note>;

<summary>The author discusses the history and persistence of witchcraft, attributing its origins to "secret sciences" from the Crusades. He lists seven common agricultural misfortunes, such as sick livestock and failed butter-making, that were traditionally blamed on sorcery.</summary>
<keywords>witchcraft, sorcerers, Crusades, folklore, livestock diseases, animal husbandry, rural life, superstition, butter churning</keywords>

### 8. That beer will not brew.

The lightheadedness with which people nowadays reject everything prevents many from paying attention to these effects; and through that, from finding the means to avert them. Some harm themselves through superstition, while others harm themselves through disbelief.

I will propose here remedies against this, based on the very nature of the matter; however, I have judged it best not to describe the methods by which these mischiefs are produced, so as not to give evil people an occasion to use them to the harm of their neighbor.

## On the Language of Animals.

Language is the expression of feelings. Animals feel and express their feelings: consequently, they also speak.

The perfection of language consists in the quality of the expression of feelings. Language alone, says <note>Johann Gottfried von Herder (1744–1803) was an influential German philosopher who argued that language is the organ of thought and the defining characteristic of the human species.</note> Herder, made man into man, confining the impulses of his passions within limits and establishing through words monuments for the intellect.

As man is the most perfect creature on earth: so too is his language the most perfect of all.

The language of animals is insufficient, proportionate to their purpose and needs: nevertheless, it too is a language.

Animals express their feelings through sounds; these sounds change according to the quality of their passions and sensations. We hear one sound from them in joy...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on animal language from page 248, transitioning from the general theory of expression to specific examples of animal behavior, specifically contrasting the simple, needs-based communication of a dog with the complex and often vain nature of human speech.</meta>

[joy]<note>This is the conclusion of the word "joy" (радости) from the previous page.</note>, another in their illness, another in anger, another in love: consequently, the sounds of their voices differ, and one who pays attention can understand them.

Vanity, arrogance, pride, and haughtiness will never be the subjects of their conversations; they express only their desires, which are limited and few in number. A dog will never complain that her kennel is not gilded; she will not demand that her food be served to her on golden platters; she will not wish to be made a commander over other dogs. She requires only food. When you are angry with her, she <term>fawns</term><note>original: ласкается (laskaetsya). To seek affection or show submission through physical contact.</note> upon you; when you leave her alone, she pines for you; you return, and she rejoices. She is an enemy to your enemies; she tells you when a stranger enters, chases after game, and reveals its location to you. To these boundaries extend the limits of her language with man.

Not all animals have a vocal language; many express themselves through movements alone. Even the dog tries to supplement the expression [of her feelings] with body movements...

[of her] feelings, so that we might understand her better. What <term>contortions</term><note>original: извороты (izvoroty).
