Of all animals, birds are the most talkative, and the following theory serves to help understand them.

# THEORY OF THE LANGUAGE OF ANIMALS.

## *Assumptions.*

Beasts do not have "words" in the strict sense; that is, they do not signify every individual object with a unique variation of the voice. Their expressions consist for the most part of many tones; thus, what constitutes an entire phrase for a human is often only a single "word" for them.

The language of beasts is the expression of their true feelings: consequently, it is very simple, proportionate to their needs (which are not numerous), and truthful; for it is the expression of genuine emotion. A bird does not tell another that she loves her when she does not, or that she rejoices when she is not glad.

<margin>theory of animal language, feelings, birds, tones, phrase</margin>

<summary>The author concludes that dogs use body language to supplement their limited vocal range and introduces a theory of animal language, asserting that animal communication is simpler than human speech but fundamentally more honest.</summary>

<keywords>animal linguistics, bird song, canine body language, emotional expression, sincerity in nature, vocal tones vs. words</keywords>

--- Page 271 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Theory of the Language of Animals" from page 250. While the previous page established that animal language is truthful and tied to physical needs, this page focuses on the repetitive nature of these communications and how they mirror the duration of an animal's emotional state.</meta>

251

The language of animals is repeated as long as their feeling continues. A wounded dog yelps loudly as long as the pain is intense.

And so, the language of animals consists of incessant repetitions. A person wishing to understand them must learn the variations of an animal's voice according to their <term>passions</term><note>original: страстямъ (strastyam). In this 18th-19th century context, "passions" refers to strong internal drives or emotional states like fear, anger, or maternal instinct, rather than just romantic love.</note>: then he will understand their language.

Thus, for example, a dog, continuing its barking at night, always barks in one manner when it wants to say: *Beware!—A stranger has come!—he has not left yet—beware!* And in this way, it repeats incessantly until its sensation and concern are quieted.

The same is true for birds, whom I have observed very closely. Their monosyllabic and disyllabic abrupt tones express their concern.

Observe a dog when she cares for her puppies; also a cat and birds, for example, a hen; especially if she was set upon duck eggs and hatched ducklings. The ducklings swim; while the mother, running <note>The text cuts off mid-sentence as the author describes the hen's frantic behavior when her "children" (the ducklings) take to the water.</note>

<summary>The author explains that animal communication relies on repetition to signal the duration of a feeling and provides examples of how domestic animals use specific vocal tones to express concern or alarm.</summary>

<keywords>animal psychology, canine communication, repetition in nature, maternal instinct, bird calls, dog barking, passions of animals</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of animal language from page 251, specifically completing the anecdote about a hen raising ducklings before moving on to broader observations of avian communication and a historical anecdote about Apollonius of Tyana.</meta>

252

along the shore with anxiety, she produces disyllabic sounds, which are the expressions of her concern. All birds, upon seeing a bird of prey <note>original: хищную птицу (khishchnuyu ptitsu)</note>, express their anxiety in the same manner.

On the contrary, quick harmonic tones following one after another are the expression of pleasure and joy; for example, the lark expresses its joy at the onset of morning.

Quick, indistinct, hurried tones are a notification of something good; for instance, a sparrow, upon seeing food, notifies its brothers of it.

It is told of **Apollonius of Tyana** <note>A Greek Neopythagorean philosopher from the 1st century AD who was reputed to have miraculous powers, including the ability to understand the speech of animals.</note> that once, while standing before a house and listening diligently to sparrows, he announced that he had heard from them that some grain had been spilled nearby, and one of them had brought the news to the others: everyone was astonished to find that this was indeed the case.

Tones of love among birds are quite distinct from tones of joy; they are tenderer and more harmonious: one need only listen to birds pairing <note>original: понимающихся (ponimayushchikhsya). In this context, it refers to the spring ritual of birds "understanding" one another or mating.</note> in the spring.

<summary>The author describes how different vocal rhythms in birds signify specific emotions like alarm, joy, or the discovery of food, citing the philosopher Apollonius of Tyana as a historical example of someone who understood these signals.</summary>

<keywords>ornithology, bird song, Apollonius of Tyana, animal communication, animal behavior, sparrows, larks, predator alarms</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the analysis of animal vocalizations (anger and sadness) and transitions into a new chapter discussing the metaphysical properties of words, herbs, and stones, bridging the gap between ancient mysticism and natural science.</meta>

Tones of anger <note>original: гнѣвъ (gnev)</note> are sharp, piercing, and hurriedly follow one another without harmony: if they continue for a long time, they signify jealousy <note>original: ревность (revnost)</note>.

Tones of sadness and longing <note>original: печаль, тоска (pechal, toska)</note> are monosyllabic and piercing.

He who notices this, examines it, and looks deeply into it, can understand the language of animals.

# ON WORDS, HERBS, AND STONES.

->*In words, herbs, and stones.* <note>original Latin: "In verbis, herbis et lapidibus." This was a popular medieval and Renaissance aphorism suggesting that all healing and magic in the world derived from these three sources.</note><-

<margin>p</margin>
The Ancients said that great powers are hidden in words, herbs, and stones.

Some laughed at this saying, while others took it literally; and both the former and the latter were mistaken.

Generally speaking, the Ancients were right: but we, having failed to understand them, have perceived it in an entirely different light. In ancient times, due to a lack of knowledge of Nature <note>original: Натуры (Natury)</note>, superstition and extravagance led people astray. Indeed, there is a special science regarding the miraculous powers of words, herbs, and stones, and the Divine Wisdom <note>original: Премудрость Божія (Premudrost Bozhiya). This term often refers to "Sophia," the personification of divine knowledge in mystical traditions.</note> teaches it to those who fear Him.

Words have an extraordinary power over the human heart when they

<summary>This page concludes the study of animal emotions expressed through sound and introduces a philosophical discussion on the hidden powers of language, botany, and mineralogy as understood by the ancients.</summary>
<keywords>animal language, anger, sadness, ancient wisdom, Divine Wisdom, mysticism, linguistics, natural philosophy, In verbis herbis et lapidibus</keywords>

--- Page 274 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from the previous page regarding the "miraculous powers of words, herbs, and stones," moving from the theory of language into a critique of mineral magic and an introduction to the study of plants.</meta>

254

are spoken from the soul <note>original: душа (dusha)</note>. Every word is an imprint of our feeling; it is powerful and effective when the power of the soul pours out into it.

There are words of expression, words of soulful sensations, and words of powers <note>This likely refers to the "theurgic" or magical tradition where certain words were believed to have the inherent power to command spirits or nature.</note>.

When an inspired person speaks, wonder gathers crowds around him; his feeling becomes the feeling of others, his sensation their sensation.

From words, tears flow and are dried. He who knows the power of words will encourage the despondent and tame the angry.

Stones in ancient times were used not only for healing, but also for various miraculous actions: but the miracles recounted about them by the ancients were rarely confirmed by experience. Albert the Great <note>Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280), a medieval polymath whose writings on the "virtues" of minerals were highly influential.</note> and Albert the Lesser <note>Refers to the *Petit Albert*, a famous 18th-century grimoire of folk magic and alchemy that was falsely attributed to Albertus Magnus.</note> wrote much that is false regarding this: but in other authors, there is much that is true in places. The curious will not lose their labor if they read them: but we shall proceed to the description of herbs.

Herbs have various properties. <margin>[</margin> He who wishes to know their power must study <term>Botany</term> <note>The scientific study of plants, which the author distinguishes from the "superstition" mentioned on the previous page.</note>. I will show here only a brief classification of them.

->In the kingdom of plants, it is generally observed:<-

1. Greenery, herbs, stems, flowers, garden vegetables and plants, field and garden fruits, shrubs, trees, and fungi.

Flowers have a root, a stem, leaves, a <term>calyx</term> <note>The green outer part of a flower that protects the bud.</note>, a <term>corolla</term> <note>The ring of petals.</note>, vessels, a pistil, a tongue, a head, and a stalk.

In fruits: a twig, skin or shell, the body <note>The fleshy part of the fruit.</note>, and the kernel or seed.
In seeds: the grain, the skin, the kernel, and the sprout.

#### 1. Into the *bitter*.

c.) break up <term>obstructions</term> <note>In historical medicine, "obstructions" referred to blockages in the internal organs or the flow of bodily "humors."</note>.

### WEIGHT, MEASURE, AND NUMBER.

#### Ancient opinion of the learned:

> <note>original Latin: "Pondere, mensura, numero Deus omnia fecit."</note> God created all things by weight, measure, and number,

->that is:<-

> By weight, measure, and number
> The Creator made the universe

In weight, measure, and number lies the connection of all things.

The sciences leading to these insights are Chemistry, Mechanics, and Arithmetic. These sciences are necessary for the Investigator of the mysteries of <term>Nature</term> <note>original: Натуры (Natury) — used here in the sense of the physical world or the cosmos.</note>.

He who knows the nature of the constituent parts, and knows how to decompose things and join them again according to weight, measure, and number: he shall produce great wonders.

For the investigating mind, it is sufficient if it knows the means of <term>dissolving bodies</term> <note>original: разрѣшенія тѣлъ (razresheniya tel) — in an alchemical or chemical sense, this refers to the process of breaking a solid substance down into its component parts or into a liquid state using a solvent.</note>. W a t e r dissolves or loosens acidic, alkaline, neutral, earthy, metallic, and oily salts; gums, vegetable and animal fluids and <term>phlegms</term> <note>original: мокроты (mokroty) — in historical medicine and chemistry, this refers to various bodily secretions or viscous fluids.</note>; acidic and alkaline soapy substances, moist herbal extracts, <term>liver of sulfur</term> <note>original: сѣрную печенку (sernuyu pechenku) — a historical term for a mixture of potassium sulfides, named for its liver-like color.</note>, <term>liver of antimony</term> <note>original: сурмяную печенку (surmyanuyu pechenku) — an alchemical preparation of antimony.</note>, and quartzes.

<term>Ether</term> <note>original: Эѳиръ (efir) — specifically diethyl ether, known for its high volatility and ability to dissolve substances that water cannot.</note> dissolves: essential oils, <term>elastic resin</term> <note>original: эластическую смолу (elasticheskuyu smolu) — an early term for natural rubber or caoutchouc.</note>, the resinous and ethereal parts of plants, acidic and alkaline <term>saponaceous matters</term> <note>original: мыловатости (mylovatosti) — substances with soap-like properties, often referring to compounds of fats with acids or alkalis.</note>, liver of sulfur, phosphorus, balsams, camphor, and in certain cases, gold.

<term>Essential oil</term> <note>original: Эѳирное масло (efirnoye maslo) — volatile oils distilled from plants, such as turpentine or oil of lavender.</note> dissolves: resin, sulfur, camphor, wax, fatty oils, acidic and alkaline saponaceous matters, balsams, liver of sulfur, certain metallic bodies, and the coloring parts of plants.

<term>Dulcified acids</term> <note>original: Ослащенныя кислоты (oslashchennyya kisloty) — historical chemical term for acids mixed with alcohol (spirits), which "sweetened" or softened their corrosive nature, such as dulcified spirit of nitre.</note> dissolve: ether, essential oils, oily salts, camphor, resinous and ethereal-oily plants, and acidic and alkaline saponaceous matters.

Fatty substances, or <insert>pressed oils</insert>, dissolve: resins, sulfur, camphor, wax, fat, balsams, acidic and alkaline saponaceous matters, <term>calx of lead</term> <note>original: свинцовую известь (svintsovuyu izvest) — lead oxide, created by heating the metal in air until it turns to a powder.</note>, copper, liver of sulfur, and the coloring parts of plants.

<term>Hydrochloric acid</term> <note>original: Соляная кислота (solyanaya kislota) — literally "salt acid."</note> dissolves: fixed and volatile alkaline salts, <term>absorbent earths</term> <note>original: всасывающія земли (vsasyvayushchiya zemli) — minerals like chalk or magnesia that were known to "absorb" or neutralize acids.</note>, copper, iron, tin, zinc, <term>regulus of antimony</term> <note>original: сурьмяной королекъ (surmyanoy korolek) — the pure metallic form of antimony, separated from its ore.</note>, <term>marcasite</term> <note>original: марказитъ — a term then used broadly for various iron sulfide minerals like pyrite.</note>, nickel, and metallic precipitates.

<summary>This page continues the technical classification of solvents, detailing the specific dissolving properties of ether, essential oils, dulcified acids, fatty oils, and hydrochloric acid. It serves as a practical guide for the chemical decomposition of organic and inorganic materials, including metals and resins.</summary>

### Main Principles

Every body has its immutable laws, by which it is what it is, and through the change of which it is itself changed.

Every body has its constituent parts, and every constituent part is itself a body of its own kind.

Constituent particles are divided into <term>proximate and remote</term> <note>original: ближайшія и отдаленнѣйшія — in early modern science, proximate parts were the immediate components (like salt and water in brine), while remote parts were the ultimate fundamental elements or atoms.</note>.

<summary>This page concludes the catalog of solvents by detailing the properties of vinegar, ammonia, animal acids, and caustic bases. It then introduces a new section on the fundamental "Main Principles" of matter, defining the laws of physical identity and the nature of constituent particles.</summary>

<keywords>vinegar, volatile alkalis, animal acid, caustic alkalis, spermaceti, Prussian blue, physical laws, constituent parts, proximate particles, remote particles</keywords>

--- Page 285 ---
<meta>This page elaborates on the definitions of proximate and remote constituent parts introduced at the end of page 264, using the chemical composition of cinnabar as a primary example, and begins a discussion on the inherent and accidental forces of matter.</meta>

265

<term>Proximate parts</term> <note>original: ближайшія (blizhaishiya) — the immediate components of a mixture or compound.</note> are the first constituent parts of a body; and <term>remote parts</term> <note>original: отдаленнѣйшія (otdalennyeishiya) — the fundamental elements that make up the proximate parts.</note> are the constituent parts of the constituent parts, for example: the proximate constituent parts of <term>cinnabar</term> <note>original: киноварь (kinovar) — a bright red mineral consisting of mercury sulfide, the primary ore of mercury.</note> are sulfur and <term>mercury</term> <note>original: меркурій (merkuriy) — quicksilver.</note>; while the remote parts would be the constituent parts of the sulfur itself.

A change in the remote constituent parts always changes the proximate constituent parts of a body: but a change in the proximate does not always change the remote.

When the arrangement of the constituent parts is disrupted, the body may be destroyed; but the remote constituent parts may suffer nothing at all from that. These remote parts, however, cannot be changed without the body itself being transformed.

The various qualities of bodies arise from the different proportions of their composition. Every body has *forces properly belonging to it and accidental forces.*

Proper forces consist for the most part in the relationship of the remote constituent parts; while *accidental* forces consist in the relationship of the form.

And so, with a change of the remote constituent parts, the proper <note>The sentence continues onto the next page.</note>

<summary>This page explains the hierarchy of matter through "proximate" and "remote" parts, using cinnabar (mercury and sulfur) as an example. it establishes that while a body's outward form may change, its fundamental remote particles remain stable unless the body's entire nature is altered.</summary>

<keywords>proximate parts, remote parts, cinnabar, mercury, sulfur, chemical composition, proper forces, accidental forces, atomic theory, early modern chemistry</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 265 regarding "proper" and "accidental" forces. It uses the examples of magnets and geometric shapes (spheres and cubes) to distinguish between the essential nature of a substance and its outward physical properties.</meta>

proper forces; while with a change of forms, the accidental ones change.

The action of attraction in an artificial <term>magnet</term> <note>original: магнитъ (magnit). In the 18th-century context, "artificial" often referred to iron that had been magnetized by a lodestone, rather than a naturally occurring magnetic mineral.</note> is an accidental force; for it arises from the arrangement of parts, which are nothing other than form, and do not belong to the [essential] composition of the body.

A person can change the accidental forces of a body without producing a change in the internal forces regarding the essence of the body. <term>Gold</term> <note>original: золото (zoloto).</note> remains entirely gold, whether in the shape of a sphere or in the shape of a cube.

The stronger attracts the weaker, the weaker [attracts] the lightest.

Hardness and weakness, lightness and heaviness consist partly in the constituent parts of bodies, and partly in their forms.

Every body can have true hardness and accidental [hardness], true weakness and accidental [weakness]. In this way, a leaden sphere is accidentally weaker <note>In this context, "weakness" (слабость) refers to a lack of resistance to movement or change in state.</note> than a leaden cube, and a cube is accidentally harder than a sphere; because a sphere is easier to set in motion than a cube.

He who discovers the constituent parts of a body, their measure, forces, and forms: he <note>The sentence continues onto the next page.</note>

<summary>This page distinguishes between the inherent properties of matter (proper forces) and those dictated by shape or arrangement (accidental forces). It explains that while a substance's essence remains the same (e.g., gold), its physical behavior, such as magnetic attraction or ease of movement, depends on its form.</summary>

267

...can also change their actions. By learning why <term>fire</term> <note>original: огонь (ogon). In the chemistry of this period, fire was often considered a physical element or substance that resided within bodies.</note> burns and a body is consumed by fire, I can make what is combustible incombustible.

The action of every body depends partly on the body itself, and partly on the objects upon which it acts.

There is no action without reaction; in every action there are <term>acting parts</term> and <term>suffering parts</term> <note>original: страждущія части (strazhdushchiya chasti). This term, derived from the same root as "passion," refers to the passive elements of matter that receive an influence or force, as opposed to the "acting" parts that exert it.</note>.

When the measure of suffering parts in any body exceeds the measure of the acting parts: then the body in which the acting parts exceed the suffering ones can act upon it.

The measure of action is proportional to the capacity of the suffering parts. Every action of one body upon another is a certain kind of communication of its own forces. Equal forces are not communicated: whereas unequal ones produce actions.

The action corresponds to the constituent parts. So long as it continues, there is no <term>equilibrium</term> <note>original: равновѣсіе (ravnovesiye).</note> of forces between the bodies acting upon one another. This equilibrium seeks to restore itself, and from that arises <term>fermentation</term> <note>original: броженіе (brozheniye). In 18th-century natural philosophy, "fermentation" was often used to describe any internal agitation, chemical reaction, or "ebullition" of particles seeking a stable state, rather than just the biological process of yeast.</note> and motion.

<summary>This page explores the mechanics of interaction between bodies, defining action and reaction through the relationship of "acting" and "suffering" parts. It posits that motion and internal agitation (fermentation) are the results of forces seeking to restore a state of equilibrium.</summary>

--- Page 288 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 267 regarding the interaction of forces and the movement of particles seeking equilibrium.</meta>

268

Like seeks like, and equal seeks equal. The force of <term>likeness-being</term> <note>original: подобобытіе (podobobytiye). This term refers to an inherent principle of assimilation, where a substance strives to bring surrounding matter into harmony with its own nature or essence.</note> found in every body fights with foreign parts until it makes them like itself, or casts them out; and consequently, until it changes the body or makes it something else entirely.

When there is no possibility of becoming like [the other], then the struggle of the competing forces continues until the particles of the body dissolve and the unlike is separated.

With these principles, one can explain all the wonders of Chemistry and Mechanics; from these principles, one can draw conclusions and derive consequences.

### ON NUMBERS

The science of numbers is one of the most important subjects of <term>Magic</term> <note>In the 18th-century context of this manuscript, "Magic" refers to "Natural Magic"—the study of the hidden or "occult" laws of nature and the mathematical patterns underlying the physical world.</note>. It reveals the true laws of things. With its help, one learns the magnitude, extension, and measure of all bodies.

This science consists of:
addition,
subtraction,
multiplication,

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the science of numbers from page 268, transitioning from the basic operations of arithmetic to the philosophical nature of the "unit" or the number one.</meta>

269

...from the sum, or product:
which is divided into
Arithmetic and Geometric.

The science of numbers is the study of the laws of <term>plurality</term> <note>Russian: множество (mnozhestvo). In 18th-century natural philosophy, this refers to the study of "the many"—how individual units combine to form complex systems.</note>.

The composition of numbers, it seems, continues to infinity; however, it is not infinite.

From a present known number, man can reach the discovery of a future unknown number, or a past one.

Consequently, in calculation there is the possibility, through the present, to find the future or the past, and to make it present through comparison or <term>proportion</term> <note>Russian: содержанію (soderzhaniyu). In this mathematical context, it refers to the ratio or relationship between values.</note>.

Numbers are nothing other than repetitions of the <term>unit</term> <note>Russian: единица (yedinitsa). This refers to the number "One" or the "Monad," considered the indivisible source of all other numbers.</note>.

The unit is unchangeable; it has no parts, it is multiplied only by itself, and its product is also the unit.

It is the beginning and the end of all numbers; yet it itself has neither beginning nor end.

It is itself the symbol of itself, the symbol of unity and harmony.

<summary>This page explores the philosophical foundations of mathematics, defining numbers as repetitions of a single "unit." It suggests that through the laws of calculation and proportion, one can use known values to uncover unknown truths about the past and future.</summary>

<keywords>arithmetic, geometry, plurality, infinity, calculation, the unit, monad, unity, harmony, 18th-century mathematics</keywords>

--- Page 290 ---
<meta>This page continues the exploration of numerical symbolism, moving from the definition of the "unit" on page 269 to the specific mystical and philosophical qualities attributed to the numbers two through eight.</meta>

270

The number 2 is the first number; for it is the first <term>plurality</term> <note>Russian: множество (mnozhestvo). In classical and esoteric philosophy, the "One" or "Unit" was often considered the source of numbers rather than a number itself; therefore, "two" is the first instance of "many."</note>. Its measure is the unit, from which it originates. It is the symbol of production, creation, the number of knowledge, love, and union.

The number 3 is the first independent number and is called *holy*. It is the symbol of perfection and the first cubic number <note>In this mystical tradition, the number three is often referred to as "cubic" or "solid" because it is the first number to possess a beginning, a middle, and an end, or because it is required to define the first geometric surface (a triangle).</note>.

The number 4 is the number of bodies, a symbol of firmness; by it, the things of the corporeal world are measured <note>This likely refers to the four elements—earth, air, fire, and water—which were believed to constitute all physical matter.</note>.

The number 5 is the offspring of the first odd number joined with the first even number, or the offspring of the <term>even with the odd</term> <note>In Pythagorean numerology, even numbers were considered feminine and odd numbers masculine. Five, the sum of 2 (the first even) and 3 (the first odd), represents the union of these two principles, often associated with marriage or biological life.</note>. It is the symbol of justice and union.

The number 6 is the seal of the world, a symbol of perfection and contentment. It is also called the human number, and the number of labor and service <note>A reference to the Biblical Hexameron, the six days of creation, after which the world was complete and "very good."</note>.

7 is the number of human life. It is called the number of birth, formation, and being, and is the symbol of knowledge, repentance, and forgiveness, and the symbol of time.

8 is the number of fulfillment and justice; a symbol of the destruction of temporal things, the number of bliss and joy.

Such was the main division of numbers among the ancients, especially among the <term>Kabbalists</term> <note>Followers of Kabbalah, a mystical tradition that originated in Judaism and was later adopted by Christian occultists. But the mystery of numbers suffered the same fate as other mysteries: we did not understand it—and we rejected it.

->P R I N C I P L E S.<-

Numbers are the distinguishing signs of a multitude of identical objects. When we want to signify a multitude of identical objects, we *enumerate*, or *calculate*. Without numbers, man cannot determine the quantity of identical objects.

The names of numbers are arbitrary, like all words: but the numbers themselves <note>The text cuts off mid-word (на-) as the sentence continues onto the next page.</note>

<meta>This page continues the sentence from page 271, completing the thought that while names of numbers are arbitrary, the numbers themselves exist as objective realities. This section explores the "Principles" of arithmetic from a philosophical, almost mystical perspective.</meta>

272

...are found in the nature of things, and not in the human will.

The result, or product, of many numbers added together is necessary, and existed in the very essence of the numbers even before they were added.

Numbers through union multiply and give birth to others, without losing anything themselves.

For example, 5 is a number in itself: but 3 and 2, 4 and 1, are its parts: yet its true parts are units; for 5 signifies 5 <term>units</term> <note>The "unit" (Russian: *edinitsa*) refers to the number one, viewed here as the fundamental building block of all reality.</note>. And so, the breaking down of every number into its constituent parts will again produce the unit.

The unit remains a unit in its essence: but in relation to other numbers, it changes its essence, and the <term>relation</term> <note>The original Russian *soderzhanie* (содержаніе) literally means "content" or "inclusion," but in this context describes the relationship or ratio between units that forms a new number.</note> of the first unit to the second is called 2; the relation of one unit to two units 3; of two to one also 3; of two to two 4; of three to one also 4; and of one to three equally 4, and so forth.

Numbers have their immutable laws. When a unit is added to any number, the number will certainly increase; and when a unit is taken away, it will certainly decrease.

Arithmetic teaches these immutable laws, and therefore it leads us to the true knowledge of things.

Now the question follows: what can be calculated?

Answer: everything—that consists of many parts; everything that has time, space, and measure; everything that is divisible; everything that has <term>arithmetic content</term> <note>The original Russian *soderzhanie* (содержаніе) refers to the inherent mathematical properties or proportions within an object that allow it to be quantified.</note>.

I once presented a demonstration that surprised many. To an enthusiast of the lottery, I said that if I so desired, I could guess which five numbers would be drawn. "In what way can you know this?" he asked me. "I have a certain powder," I replied, "which, if I burn it <note>The original *pokuryu* (покурю) suggests burning a substance like incense to create smoke or vapors, a common trope in 18th-century "natural magic" demonstrations.</note>, the numbers will appear on the floor of my room." He began to laugh at this; and that was exactly what I wanted, so that I might surprise him all the more later. On the day the tickets were drawn, I went to him. "Why," I said, "did you not want to believe me then? You certainly would have guessed the numbers." "But show me your powder," he said. "As you wish: here it is!"

What are letters? What are numbers?

How do letters differ from numbers, and numbers from letters?

What is a <term>combination</term> <note>original: *sovokuplenie* / *combinatio*. How can I bring this combination of numbers from ninety down to five? <note>Lotteries of this era, such as the Genoese lottery, often involved drawing 5 numbers out of 90.</note>

<meta>This page transitions from the specific lottery prediction trick to broader mentalism feats involving arithmetic and word games. The author frames these as "experiments" based on fundamental mathematical principles, leading into a formal section on the theory of "magical" calculation.</meta>

->276<-

The answer is extremely simple: but many will rack their brains in vain and will not find the solution. These very rules led me to the following experiments <note>original: *opyty*. In the 18th century, this word encompassed both scientific experiments and the "demonstrations" performed by magicians.</note>.

I shall write a number on a piece of paper and seal it. Then, let someone set a task; be it addition, subtraction, multiplication, or division: the sealed number will contain the solution to this task.

I will seal a small packet, having written several letters within it. After it is sealed, let someone say what word they wish to find in the packet, even if it were a proper name: upon unsealing the packet, they will find exactly that very word.

### NECESSARY ASSUMPTIONS FOR MAGICAL AND MIRACULOUS CALCULATIONS, OR COMPUTATIONS.

The objects of calculation <note>original: *schislenie* - the act of numbering or counting</note> are—magnitude, extension, and measure.

There are two rules for magnitude—addition and subtraction.

Addition is the method of joining together many magnitudes of the same kind.

<meta>This page continues the technical definitions of arithmetic operations—subtraction, multiplication, and division—which the author presents as the underlying logic for the "magical" mentalism tricks introduced on the previous page. He transitions from basic operations to the concept of proportions and ratios.</meta>

->277<-

Subtraction teaches us to determine the difference between two magnitudes: or, what is all the same, to determine: how much of one magnitude will remain, if another is taken away from it.

Multiplication consists in finding the product of a magnitude of the same kind, increased in a specific manner. Multiplication is properly an abbreviated addition.

Division shows how many times one magnitude is contained within another magnitude of the same kind. It is an abbreviated subtraction.

The result arising from the comparison of two magnitudes is called the *Product* <note>original: *Proizvedeniye*. In modern Russian, this specifically means the result of multiplication, but the author here uses it more broadly to describe the "outcome" of any mathematical comparison.</note>, which is divided into arithmetic and geometric.

Arithmetic comparison is the difference between two magnitudes, produced through subtraction.

Geometric [comparison] is the difference between two magnitudes, produced through division.

The comparison between magnitudes and quantities is called *Proportion*.

Arithmetic proportion consists in an additive comparison: while geometric proportion consists in a quantitative comparison <note>original: *kolikago*. This is an archaic term derived from "how many," referring to the ratio or the "how-muchness" of one number relative to another.</note>.

<gloss>subtraction, multiplication, division, product/result, arithmetic, geometric, proportion</gloss>

<summary>The author provides foundational definitions for subtraction, multiplication, and division, framing them as forms of abbreviated addition and subtraction. He further distinguishes between arithmetic and geometric comparisons, leading into the definition of mathematical proportion.</summary>

<keywords>subtraction, multiplication, division, arithmetic comparison, geometric comparison, proportion, mathematical foundations, Enlightenment education</keywords>

->278<-

When a proportion consists of more than three terms, it is then called a *Progression*.

These are the ordinary rules of numbers. There are others, but they are not for everyone: one of the most excellent sciences is the knowledge of the properties of numbers.

The special properties of certain numbers are as follows:

1.) If, of any two numbers, one is divisible by 3: then the product of these numbers multiplied by each other is also divisible by 3 <note>original: *3 жъ*. The archaic particle "zh" serves to emphasize that it is the very same number 3.</note>.

2.) If two different numbers are divisible by the same number <note>meaning they share a common divisor</note>, then their difference, their sum, and their product are all divisible by that same number.

3.) Numbers which are divisible by 3, whether they are added or multiplied, produce a number that is also divisible by 3.

4.) Every number multiplied by one that is divisible by 3 gives a product that is also divisible by 3.

5.) Every number multiplied by 9, or by another number divisible by 9, produces a total <note>original: *summu*. In this context, the author uses "sum" to mean the resulting numerical value or total, where a modern reader would expect "product."</note> which is divisible by 9.

<gloss>progression, properties of numbers, divisibility</gloss>

### ON THE KABBALAH

The Kabbalah <term>Kabbalah: a form of Jewish mysticism</term> was the secret and symbolic theology of the Jews. Its chief mystery consists in the fact that the Kabbalists reduced all things of the number 3 into unity, and from this, they derived all differences and progressions in harmonic proportion.

### *The System of Creation*

->The Creator, the creature, (the link between them)<-

The art of the Kabbalist <term>Kabbalist: a practitioner of the Jewish mystical system of Kabbalah, which the author interprets here through an Enlightenment-era mathematical lens</term> consists in the knowledge of finding the essence of things according to the rules of harmony and disharmony <note>In this context, harmony and disharmony refer to the mathematical resonance or discord between the numerical values of words and concepts.</note>.

Kabbalistic calculation consists of the following:
1. Numbers from 11 to 99 are concentrated <note>This likely refers to "reduction," a common numerological practice where multi-digit numbers are added together or otherwise simplified to find a core root value.</note>;
2. Then the alphabet is reduced to 12 consonant letters;
3. Each consonant letter is assigned a number;
4. Then a question is written down;
5. It is converted into the consonant letters of the Kabbalistic alphabet;
6. Numbers are attached to the letters, and the product of each word is calculated;
7. The sum of the entire question is taken;

8. This sum is divided into 12 Kabbalistic houses <term>houses: sectors used in divination and astrology to represent different areas of human life, such as health or wealth</term>, which are called:
> Life, wealth, brothers, father, children, health,

9. There is no zero among symbolic numbers; it is always transformed into a one. A quadrangle <note>Likely a square grid or chart used to organize the calculations.</note> is constructed from the Kabbalistic houses, and the total sum is placed in the bottom row.

10. Then the quadrangle is filled: this is done by extracting the square root (the radix) <note>original: "радикса" (radix). Here, the author uses the mathematical term for a root to describe finding the fundamental numerical essence of the "question" being asked.</note> from the primary parts of the question.

11. The numbers of the extracted square roots are distributed into their houses according to their relevance, until the entire quadrangle is filled.

12. After that, the numbers are doubled and the *theme* <note>original: "тема" (task/problem). In early modern logic and mathematics, the 'theme' is the specific proposition or problem to be solved.</note> is prepared.

13. The numbers, along with their symbolic signs, are brought into the contents of the Kabbalistic houses, and triangles are drawn from them.

14. Finally, the product of the numbers is transformed back into the letters of the Kabbalistic alphabet. <note>This final step suggests that the resulting numbers are converted back into text to provide the "answer" to the original question.</note>

Whoever desires to have fundamental knowledge of this should read good Kabbalistic books, of which, admittedly, there are few that are accurate. ### THE TEMPLE OF HEALTH AND THE MIRACULOUS BED OF DOCTOR GRAHAM.
<note>Dr. James Graham (1745–1794) was a famous Scottish eccentric and physician who opened the "Temple of Health" in London. He promoted "electro-magnetism" and vitalist therapies, most famously his "Celestial Bed," which was claimed to cure infertility and restore vitality.</note>

Through the senses, man tastes the sweetness of life; they bring both pleasure and sorrow to the soul.

There are movements in the body necessary for the continuation of the animal's life <note>In the 18th century, "animal" (from the Latin *anima*, meaning breath or soul) referred to any living, breathing creature, including humans, in contrast to plants or minerals.</note>, which I shall call the movements of the animal's vital forces.

When this is not the case, then he is ill.

Blood and humors that have become too thick or too thin; fibers and nerves that are too tense or too slack; an unequal circulation of fluids <term>fluids: "vlazhnosti" (moistures) in the original; a reference to the balance of bodily liquids in Vitalist medicine</term>—these are the primary causes of both illness and sorrow.

A person cannot experience either pleasure or sorrow without his state undergoing a substantial change.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of James Graham (1745–1794) and his "Celestial Bed." Since sensual people are everywhere, and in London there are plenty of wealthy ones as well, ***Doctor Graham*** was able to grow rich from his bed and from his Temple. As for the fact that he took advantage of people's weaknesses and sensuality for his own enrichment—he cannot yet be blamed for that. When has it ever happened that an inventor received a worthy reward without resorting to some kind of charlatanry? The noble and the wealthy do not chase after reason, but after sensuality; not after knowledge, but after amusements.

Furthermore, although Mr. Graham calls his bed "magnetic-electrical" and says that nothing like it has ever existed in the world, nor ever will, I undertake to make the same thing more cheaply; for a physicist, his ***Celestial Temple*** and ***Celestial Bed*** are no mystery. It is known that—

Just as a tone falls silent when a finger leaves the key, so the color disappears as soon as the finger is removed; for the valves, by their own weight, immediately fall back and cover the vials. From behind, I illuminated this clavichord with tall candles. It is impossible to describe the beauty of the appearing colors; they surpass the most precious gemstones. This experiment is quite amusing when performed with a bird. People think a little bird is sitting on the table, and they see all its movements; but in truth, it is only its shadow. It is also amusing to perform this experiment with a candle, when people are so deceived that they attempt to light another candle from it.

### ON THE SENSE OF SMELL.

Through the sense of smell, one can also produce various amazing effects, especially by observing the following principles:

Scents are actually particles of the fragrant body <note>original: пахучаго тѣла (pakhuchago tela). In the 18th century, it was theorized that smell was a "corporeal" sense, meaning physical microscopic particles had to physically travel from the object to the nose.</note>, and therefore they rarely lose its properties.

The stronger scent of one body alters the scent of another weaker one. And thus, by means of it, one can change the property of that very body.

Two bodies whose scents are of equal strength do not mix and do not change.

In the struggle of scents between two different bodies, if neither overcomes the other, a third scent is produced, which is characteristic of neither the one nor the other body.

A body loses its scent when all the sulfurous particles <term>sulfurous particles: in early modern chemistry and alchemy, "sulfur" was one of the primary principles of matter, representing the volatile, oily, and flammable component that carried odors.</term> have flown out of it.

Oils retain scents the longest because the sulfurous particles...

<summary>The author concludes the section on optics and begins a new chapter on the science of smell, outlining several axioms regarding how scents interact, how they are composed of physical particles, and the role of "sulfurous" elements in retaining odor.</summary>

<keywords>olfaction, sense of smell, 18th-century chemistry, sulfurous particles, physics of scent, scent interaction, natural philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 324 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of scent chemistry from page 303, specifically focusing on how different substances retain or lose smells, before transitioning to a new chapter on the "Theory of Touch."</meta>

...are bound more strongly in oil. However, scent is lost most quickly in spirits of wine <note>original: винномъ спиртѣ (vinnom spirte). This refers to ethanol or high-proof alcohol.</note>.

Heat and fire are harmful to scents, for they increase the volatility of the sulfur <note>As established previously, "sulfur" here refers to the volatile principle of a substance that carries its odor.</note>.

If one adds sulfur to fragrant water in the proper proportion, it retains the scent longer.

All alcoholic scented waters are harmful to man, while those composed with vinegar are beneficial. <note>In the 18th century, vinegar-based "smelling salts" or "four thieves vinegar" were believed to have medicinal and protective properties against disease, whereas spirits were often viewed with more caution in delicate mixtures.</note>

Saltpeter <note>original: Селитра (selitra). Also known as potassium nitrate.</note> also retains fragrance; for as it contains within itself the purest dephlogisticated air <term>dephlogisticated air: a term from the late 18th-century phlogiston theory of chemistry, later identified as oxygen. It was believed to be "pure" air that could support combustion and life better than common air.</term>, which greedily absorbs the flammable air <note>original: горючій воздухъ (goryuchiy vozdukh). Likely referring to hydrogen or the theoretical substance "phlogiston."</note>, the sulfurous particles do not separate from bodies so quickly.

### Theory of Touch.

Nerves are spread under the skin throughout the whole body and are divided into a countless multitude of branches, ending finally in little papillae <term>papillae: the author uses the term "бородавочки" (borodavochki), literally "little warts," to describe the sensory nerve endings on the skin that we now call dermal papillae.</term>, which properly constitute the organ of touch.

And therefore, touch consists in the action of bodies upon these sensitive papillae according to the properties of these papillae...

<summary>The author concludes the study of smell by explaining how substances like vinegar and saltpeter (via "dephlogisticated air") help retain scents, then introduces the anatomy of touch, focusing on nerves and skin papillae.</summary>

<keywords>olfaction, spirits of wine, saltpeter, dephlogisticated air, phlogiston theory, anatomy, nerves, sense of touch, papillae</keywords>

--- Page 325 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Theory of Touch" from page 304, describing the physical structure of the sensory papillae and proposing that all five senses are ultimately variations of the sense of touch.</meta>

...which for this purpose must be moist and plump.

Short and dry papillae <term>papillae: as noted on the previous page, the author uses "бородавочки" (borodavochki), literally "little warts," to describe the sensory nerve endings under the skin.</term> have almost no sensation; sensation itself varies according to the number and shape of these papillae. Where touch is strong and lively, there they are larger. On the tongue and on the tips of the fingers, they are visible even to the naked eye when the skin is peeled away; under the nails, they are elongated and somewhat sharp; on the cheeks and lips, they are fuzzy; and on the tongue, they resemble an inverted cone, and for the most part, an irregular one.

In the act of touch, the nerves are variously pressed down, lifted, shaken, or irritated.

1. Every sensation arises from contact with the sensitive papillae.
2. All the senses are, in essence, touch: whether with the eyes, the nose, the ear, the tongue, or the hand—I am touching; I am only touching. <note>This reflects a common 18th-century philosophical and physiological theory that all sensory perception is a specialized form of physical contact or "impact" upon the nerves.</note>
3. According to the differences in the sensitive papillae, the touch is also different.
4. The variation in the sensitive papillae produces the variety of sensations.

<summary>The author describes the physical characteristics of sensory papillae across different parts of the body and argues that all five senses are fundamentally different modes of the sense of touch.</summary>
<keywords>anatomy, sense of touch, papillae, nerves, sensory perception, physiology, Enlightenment science</keywords>

--- Page 326 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Theory of Touch" from page 305. Having established that all senses are forms of touch, the author now explains why different people perceive the same objects differently based on their physical "organization" and the state of their sensory papillae.</meta>

5. Thus, according to the difference in organization <note>In 18th-century science, "organization" referred to the specific physical structure and arrangement of an organism's parts which determined its function.</note>, sensations are also different: and for this reason, what one person likes, another does not; and what is tasty to one is repulsive to another.

6. Identical sensitive papillae <term>papillae: the author continues to use "бородавочки" (borodavochki), or "little warts," to describe the nerve endings on the skin and tongue.</term> produce identical perceptions and sensations.

7. A change in the sensitive papillae changes perceptions and sensations.

8. The change in the sensitive papillae is either essential or accidental. <note>These terms are used in the philosophical sense: "essential" refers to the inherent nature of the body, while "accidental" refers to temporary or external circumstances.</note>

9. Essential, based on the nature of the structure of the particles; accidental, based on their variability.

10. This variability is either external or internal.

11. External, when the sensitive papillae are changed by external objects; internal, when these sensitive papillae receive a different direction <note>Likely referring to the "disposition" or state of the nerve endings.</note> from the body's humors <term>humors: "соки" (soki), literally "juices," referring to the fluids of the body such as lymph or bile which were thought to influence health and temperament.</term> and blood, as for example in illnesses.

12. The sensitive particles can receive this direction either naturally or artificially.

<summary>The author explains that individual differences in taste and sensation are caused by the physical structure of sensory papillae and their "internal" or "external" changes, particularly during illness.</summary>
<keywords>subjectivity, sensation, papillae, humors, physiology, organization, perception</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the variability of sensory perception. The previous page established that sensations change based on whether the "sensitive papillae" (nerve endings) are affected by external objects or internal "humors" (body fluids).</meta>

14. Pain that arises naturally can be quelled artificially; this is exactly what a <term>physician: "лекарь" (lekar), an older term for a medical practitioner or healer.</term> does. Likewise, even when a person is in a neutral state, one can use art <note>In this context, "art" refers to medical skill, clinical application, or the "art of healing."</note> to produce joyful sensations in them. But to achieve this, one must know:
1.) Which of their parts are more sensitive?
2.) The nature of those parts' capacity for feeling and sensation.
3.) The property of their structure and their connection with other nerves and <term>fibers: "фибры" (fibry), then considered the fundamental structural threads of the body's tissues and nerves.</term>
4.) The measure of their vibration <note>The original term "потрясенія" (potryaseniya) refers to the physical shaking or oscillation of the nerves upon contact.</note> and the ratio of the acting force of the sense to the proportion of the affected parts.

Nature has hidden great mysteries within her treasuries. ...for the preservation of the animal being. Man, according to his body, is an animal, but according to his immortal soul, he is a spirit; and therefore he has a twofold capacity to feel pleasure and sorrow, both bodily and spiritual. Feeling and sensation can often be united in man. We see the transition of bodily feeling into spiritual sensation in love and friendship; and we see the transition of spiritual sensation into bodily feeling in <term>melancholics: "меланхолики" (melankholiki), individuals suffering from "melancholy," which was historically viewed as a physical disorder of the bile that resulted in profound spiritual sadness.</term> and those in love, in whom spiritual sensations become the cause of bodily diseases. <note>The author is describing what we would now call psychosomatic illness—where the mind's state affects the body's health.</note>

In weak souls, bodily sensations can take the upper hand over spiritual ones, but in firm souls, the spiritual prevail over the bodily. Thus, the virtuous man is firm in misfortune, patient in sickness, and steadfast at the moment of death.

Every man seeks pleasure; the difference lies only in the fact that some seek the true, while others seek the false. True pleasure consists in pure spiritual sensations; false pleasure in merely sensual ones. The main cause of this temperamental inclination arises, for the most part, from our parents—from the <term>blood and juices: "крови и соковъ" (krovi i sokov), a reference to the archaic physiological theory of "humors" which were believed to determine a person's health and character.</term> which they infused into us at our conception, and from the circumstances in which the mother found herself during the first days of pregnancy.

Every person must be made to understand that the act of procreation should be regarded as the most important work of humanity, to which we should be led not by mere animal lust, but by a higher, more noble feeling. Our most holy religion joins people for this work through the sacred sacrament of marriage, and its commandments are: moderation, prudent restraint, and purity of morals. ...reaches the most delicate sensitive papillae of taste: from which is born a disordered impulse, because the fibers are deranged. <term>Papillae: "бородавочекъ" (borodavochek), literally "little warts," used here to describe the taste buds on the tongue.</term> In this state, if another nerve is touched by some other sense—the eye, the ear, or the smell—and through that some idea is excited, then the sick woman seizes upon this idea and joins it with the sense of taste, which is excited most quickly of all. 

When, in this state, a pregnant woman passes, for example, by a coachmaker, feeling a desire without knowing for what, and the smell of tar hits her nose, then her sense of smell settles upon this scent. <note>Coachmakers used birch tar to grease wheels and treat leather, a pungent smell common in 18th-century cities.</note> She will not let go of this desire until she satisfies it, or is cured of it in some other way. In this case, she is like that madman who imagined that he had a clock in his head, and who was cured of this fantasy only when he was finally convinced that the clock had been removed from his head. <note>The author compares "pica" (craving non-food items) to a psychological delusion or "fixed idea."</note>

Passions and inclinations differ from each other only in degree: passion is nothing other than an inclination put into activity, or a noticeable degree of desire or aversion acting within us.

The effects of passions are manifold, and the <term>Researcher of Nature</term><note>The original term "Испытатель Натуры" (Ispytatel' Natury) refers to what we would now call a natural philosopher or a scientist—someone dedicated to observing and analyzing the laws of the physical and psychological world.</note> must study them.

Inclinations are divided into pleasant, unpleasant, and mixed.

The strongest are: grief, anger, fear, terror, courage, fury, love, boredom, <term>longing</term><note>The Russian word used here is "тоска" (toska), a term denoting a deep, often objectless spiritual anguish or melancholy.</note>, envy, hostility, hope, despondency, and despair.

Every inclination has degrees; for example: regret can produce grief; despondency and, so to speak, a sinking of the spirit are internal consequences; while fatigue and exhaustion of the body are its external consequences.

Their progression is as follows:

| | |
|---|---|
| restlessness, | fatigue, |
| regret, | weakness, |
| vexation, | exhaustion, |
| chagrin, | total debility. |

First, it is necessary to know man in general; then examine him in different circumstances and in different passions; observe the course of his thoughts; search for the causes acting upon his soul; consider what is produced in him by the <term>climate</term><note>Continuing from the previous page, this refers to the 18th-century "theory of climates" which posited that physical environment dictated cultural and personal character.</note>, the air, the food, his <term>temperament</term>, his <term>rank</term><note>The word "zvanie" refers to one’s social station or estate, which in the 18th century was believed to deeply influence an individual's worldview and behavior.</note>, and his daily activities; observe him both in a state of passion and in a calm state; analyze every action he takes, and break it down into its component parts; add to that the motivating causes, and separate the necessary consequences from those that are merely accidental: in this way, you will acquire a perfect <term>knowledge of man</term><note>The author uses "poznanie cheloveka" to describe a comprehensive, quasi-scientific understanding of human psychology and morality.</note>.

# ON PSYCHOLOGICAL MYSTERIES, OR THE SCIENCE OF THE SIBYLS <note>The Sibyls were famous prophetesses in the ancient world. The author uses "Science of the Sibyls" metaphorically to describe the ability to "prophesy" or predict human behavior through deep psychological insight.</note>.

To these psychological mysteries belong:
1.) The prediction of future human actions;
2.) The knowledge of their most secret intentions;
3.) The discovery of their <term>dissimulation</term><note>The Russian "pritvorstvo" implies pretense, hypocrisy, or the wearing of a social mask to hide one's true nature.</note>;
4.) The knowledge of the order of their thoughts;

<summary>The author argues that true psychological insight leads to compassion rather than hatred for others, and introduces a "science" of reading people that allows one to predict actions and unmask hidden intentions.</summary>
<keywords>Divinity, ladder of bliss, misanthropy, psychological mysteries, Sibyls, prediction, dissimulation, Enlightenment psychology</keywords>

--- Page 350 ---
<meta>This page continues the list of "Psychological Mysteries" introduced on page 329 and begins an explanation of the mechanics of human passion using a mathematical analogy. It establishes a rationalist framework for understanding the soul's movements.</meta>

330

5.) The art of recognizing, in certain cases, their most secret thoughts;
6.) The calculation of their deeds and the consequences thereof;
7.) The consideration of conflicting moral forces in relation to allied forces <note>The author uses the language of physics or mechanics—"conflicting" (opposing) and "allied" (cooperating) forces—to describe the internal struggle between different moral impulses.</note>;
8.) The future consequences of actions.

These branches of knowledge are called Psychological Mysteries, or the *Science of the Sibyls*.

Just as a unit relates to the first number, so man relates to the impressions of the passions. His state will be that which this application <note>In this context, "application" refers to the way external impressions or passions are "applied" to or act upon the person.</note> makes of him.

3 and 4 are numbers distinct from each other, or two separate entities; the first contains 3 units, the second 4 units: but, joining together, they constitute a *third*, called 7.

So too are the *Soul* and <term>Inclination</term><note>The original "Sklonnost" refers to a natural leaning, tendency, or predisposition toward something.</note> distinct from each other, and are likewise two separate entities: but, joining together, they produce a *third* thing yet more singular, called *Passion*.

### ON MUSIC, AND THE POWER OF HARMONY OVER THE SOUL.

The swift mobility of the brain is the cause of a sharp, penetrating intellect <term>intellect</term> <note>original: "разума" (razuma); refers to the reasoning mind or the higher faculty of understanding.</note>. Pythagoras <note>A Greek philosopher (c. 570 – c. 495 BC) who taught that the universe was governed by mathematical proportions and that music could be used to heal the soul and body.</note> used to stimulate this mobility in his students in the mornings with music; and indeed, it seems to be based on the brain's great capacity for receiving impressions; for in the dull-witted, owing to the nature of their nerves, the mobility of the brain is very slight, and in fools, it is almost entirely absent.

Tones, penetrating the human soul through the ear, set the finest fibers <term>fibers</term> <note>original: "фибры" (fibry); in 18th-century physiology, these were believed to be the fundamental structural units of the body and nerves that vibrated in response to external stimuli like sound.</note> into a gentle trembling. It is well known that every passion exerts its influence through a specific tension of the fibers and nerves, and that the human soul— <note>The text ends mid-sentence at the bottom of the page, likely continuing a discussion on how the soul reacts to these physical vibrations.</note>

—the human soul remains in this state until this condition of the nerves is changed by another vibration <note>original: "потрясенія" (potryaseniya); can mean shock, vibration, or a physical shaking up of the system.</note>. 2. That the tones should tune the nerves that have been disordered by passion;
3. That this tuning should not happen all at once; rather, the transition from anger to calm, or from despondency to cheerfulness, should occur through gradual degrees of tones.

In India and Ethiopia, its teachers were the Brahmins and Gymnosophists <note>The Gymnosophists were "naked philosophers," a term used by the Greeks to describe Indian ascetics and yogis who practiced extreme self-discipline and meditation.</note>; among the Gauls, the Druids; and in Persia, Kings themselves studied it. These last ones are the highest sciences, or true <term>Magic</term> <note>original: "Магія". In this esoteric context, Magic is viewed as the "royal science" or the practical application of spiritual laws to the physical world, distinct from common superstition.</note>, of which the first degree constitutes the *<term>Polyhistor</term>* <note>original: "Полигисторъ". A scholar of multifaceted learning; a polymath.</note>, or *Man of Great Learning*, skilled in many sciences; the second is the *<term>Pansophist</term>* <note>original: "Пансофъ". From the Greek for "universal wisdom"; one who has achieved a total, unified understanding of all knowledge.</note>, or *All-Knower*, who knows all the sciences that constitute erudition; the third and final is the *<term>Magus</term>* <note>original: "Магъ". Here, the Magus is the highest spiritual initiate who acts as a bridge between the divine plan and the human world.</note>, or *Sage*, who employs all these knowledges according to the plans of the Deity for the benefit of mankind.

Very few reach this final degree of <term>Wisdom</term> <note>original: "Премудрость". While it means wisdom, in this tradition it often refers to *Sophia*, the divine wisdom that requires spiritual purity to access.</note>; for it requires a great awareness of one's own weakness and caution against acting by one's own, rather than the Divine, will. As soon as a man loses sight of this, the light in his soul immediately dims, and he turns again into a son of darkness; his concepts fall into confusion, and he forgets the sacred mysteries of which he was unworthy and which are preserved only for the seeker of Wisdom with a pure heart and in humility of spirit.

<summary>The author lists the highest spiritual sciences—knowledge of the future, thoughts, and higher beings—defining them as true Magic. He outlines a hierarchy of mastery (Polyhistor, Pansophist, Magus) and warns that this Wisdom is lost if the seeker acts out of self-will rather than Divine will.</summary>

<keywords>Magic, Polyhistor, Pansophist, Magus, Theurgy, Divine Will, Wisdom, Sophia, Enlightenment mysticism, spiritual hierarchy</keywords>

I will provide here the best authors and the best works on the natural sciences: but let everyone bear in mind that he must resemble a bee, which extracts only honey from all the flowers. <note>This metaphor of the "scholarly bee" was a popular trope in early modern literature, famously used by writers like Francis Bacon and Jonathan Swift to describe the process of synthesizing diverse sources of knowledge into a useful whole.</note>

Following these, the works of Caspar Schott, namely:

*   Hydraulic and Pneumatic Mechanics <note>original: "Mechanica hydraulico-pneumatica." This work describes various mechanical inventions using water and air pressure.</note>.
*   Universal Magic <note>original: "Magia universalis." In the 17th century, "magic" referred to the hidden wonders of nature and mechanical marvels rather than sorcery.</note>.
*   Kircher’s Pantometer <note>original: "Pantometrum Kircherianum." A description of a surveying instrument invented by Schott's mentor, Athanasius Kircher.</note>.
*   Kircher’s Ecstatic Journeys <note>original: "Itinera extatica Kircheri." A fictionalized astronomical journey through the cosmos.</note>.
*   Mathematical Course <note>original: "Cursus mathematicus."</note>.
*   Curious Physics <note>original: "Physica curiosa." An encyclopedia of natural wonders, oddities, and monsters.</note>.
*   Imperial Mathematics <note>original: "Mathesis caesarea."</note>.

Practical Arithmetic <note>original: "Arithmetica practica." A textbook on basic and advanced calculation methods.</note>.
Anatomy of Springs and Rivers <note>original: "Anatomia fontium et fluminum." A treatise on hydrography and the movement of water.</note>.
Curious Technology <note>original: "Technica curiosa." Schott's famous work describing various mechanical wonders and inventions, including early experiments with vacuums.</note>.
The School of Steganography <note>original: "Schola Steganographica." A manual on the art of secret writing and cryptography.</note>.
The Jests and Serious Matters of Nature and Art <note>original: "Joco-Seria naturae et artis." A collection of scientific puzzles, experiments, and "miracles" explained through physics.</note>.
The Mathematical Organon <note>original: "Organum mathematicum." A description of a physical device—a sort of early calculating box—invented by Athanasius Kircher to solve mathematical problems.</note>.

Giuseppe Pinetti was a celebrated 18th-century Italian stage magician who used scientific principles to create illusions.</note> |
| Solomon <note>King Solomon, traditionally viewed in the Renaissance as the ultimate master of hidden wisdom and the commander of spirits.</note> | The works of Kircher <note>Athanasius Kircher, a Jesuit polymath whose massive encyclopedic works covered everything from magnetism and music to Egyptology.</note> |
| Astaphon <note>Likely a corruption of Astrampsychus, a legendary Egyptian magus associated with dream interpretation and oracles.</note> | Halle, *On the Magical Powers of Nature* <note>original: "Галле, о магическихъ силахъ Натуры." | Tryphon <note>Could refer to an ancient Greek grammarian or a surgeon of the same name.</note> | |

It is true that many of these writers often erred and merely copied from ancient authors without their own experience and without understanding them; for this reason, caution and selection are necessary to avoid falling into error. I also consider it necessary to note that some inventions attributed by us to current times were known completely, or in part, even to the ancients. For example: **flammable air** <note>Now known as hydrogen.</note> and **dephlogisticated air** <note>Now known as oxygen; "dephlogisticated air" was the term used by 18th-century chemists like Joseph Priestley before the modern chemical revolution.</note>, the discovery of which we have boasted, were known even in antiquity. In the works of Caspar Schott, we find proposed methods for a **hot air balloon**; and in the books of *Count Gabalis* <note>A satirical 17th-century work on elemental spirits and secret sciences.</note>, a description of the effects of dephlogisticated air; while Kircher knew of the effects of **magnetism**.

<summary>This page concludes the list of authors on natural magic and scientific illusions, while providing a critical warning about the reliability of ancient texts and arguing that "modern" scientific discoveries like gases and balloons have historical precedents.</summary>

...comprising in two small books the sympathies and antipathies of various things, or natural harmonies and discords <note>original Latin: "variarum sympathias et antipathias, seu naturales concordias et discordias libellis duobus complectens." This entry completes the description of Antoine Mizauld's work from the previous page.</note>. Frankfurt, 1592. 1613.

Erasmus, *On the various sympathy and antipathy of things of all kinds* <note>original Latin: "de varia rerum omnis generis sympathia et antipathia." Likely referring to a work by Erasmus of Rotterdam or a contemporary scholar of the same name exploring how different elements of nature attract or repel one another.</note>.

The most famous and rarest book is the work of Adam Zalužanský ze Zalužan <note>A Bohemian physician and botanist (c. 1555–1613). He was one of the first to argue that botany should be studied as a distinct science from medicine.</note>, a Physician, published in Prague in 1592 and then printed a second time in Nuremberg under the title: *Three Books on Botanical Method: On the Sympathy of Plants* <note>original Latin: "Methodi herbaria libri tres de sympathia plantarum."</note>. Voigt mentions it in his *Literary Acts of Bohemia and Moravia* <note>original Latin: "acta Bohemiae et Moraviae litterariae." Adauctus Voigt was an 18th-century historian who documented the intellectual history of the Czech lands.</note>.

->Regarding swimming through the air <note>A historical term for aeronautics or flight, as early theorists often compared the movement of objects through the atmosphere to the way fish move through water.</note>, the following have written:<-

Father Lana <note>Francesco Lana de Terzi (1631–1687), an Italian Jesuit who designed an "aerial ship" lifted by vacuum spheres—the first documented scientific plan for a lighter-than-air craft.</note>.
Albert of Saxony <note>A 14th-century philosopher who theorized on the nature of the elements and atmospheric pressure.</note>.
Mendoza <note>Francisco de Mendoza (1573–1626), a Spanish Jesuit who wrote on natural philosophy.</note>.
Schott in the 5th book of his *Hydrostatic Magic* <note>original Latin: "Magia hydrostatica." Gaspar Schott’s work on the physics of fluids and air.</note>.

This same author explains the reasons for the floating of clouds in the air. He speaks of floating islands, about which Dausquius also wrote in *Earth and Water, or Floating Lands* <note>original Latin: "Terra et aqua, seu terrae fluctuantes." Claudius Dausquius (1566–1644) was a French scholar who studied strange geographical phenomena.</note>. Tournai, 1633.

To this also belong the following remarkable works:

<meta>This page continues the bibliographic list of authors associated with natural philosophy and mechanics, transitioning from specific scholars to a registry of scientific journals and periodicals that cover "secret" sciences and magic.</meta>

<page-num>366</page-num>

Georgius Agricola <note>Georgius Agricola (1494–1555) was a German scholar known as the "father of mineralogy," though his work often touched on the mechanics of air and water in mines.</note>,
Jacques Besson <note>Jacques Besson (c. 1540–1573) was a French inventor and mathematician famous for his theater of instruments and machines.</note>,
Agostino Ramelli <note>Agostino Ramelli (1531–1600) was an Italian military engineer who designed complex hydraulic and mechanical devices.</note>.

For the invention of wind-machines, or ventilators, we are indebted to the aforementioned Father Schott <note>Gaspar Schott, mentioned on the previous page as a student of Athanasius Kircher and a chronicler of mechanical wonders.</note>. In the 7th book of the 3rd part of his *Natural Magic* <note>original Latin: "Magiae naturalis."</note>, he writes about a ventilator for cooling a bedroom.

->A Registry of journals and other periodical works in which secret philosophical sciences and Magic are discussed.<-

*Physico-Medical Acts* <note>original Latin: "Acta physico-medica." A scholarly journal focused on the intersection of physical science and medicine.</note>, 1727–1754. Ten volumes.

*New Acts* <note>original Latin: "Nova acta."</note> from 1757 to 1778. Six volumes.

*Acts of the Learned* <note>original Latin: "Acta eruditorum." The first scientific journal of the German-speaking lands, published in Leipzig.</note>, Leipzig, 1685. Two volumes.

*Commentaries on accomplishments in natural science and medicine* <note>original Latin: "Commentarii de rebus in scientia naturali et medicina gestis."</note>. Leipzig, 1752–1781. 24 parts in octavo <note><term>octavo</term> — a book size (abbreviated as "in 8.") resulting from folding a printed sheet three times to form eight leaves.</note> and 3 volumes of supplements.

*Miscellany of the Academy of the Curious in Nature*, or *Medical-Physical Curious Miscellany of the Academy of the Curious in Nature*, or *Acts of the Centuries and Decades of Medical-Physical Curious Ephemerides* <note>original Latin: "Academiae naturae curiosae miscellanea..." These titles refer to the various iterations of the journals published by the Leopoldina, the oldest scientific society in the German-speaking world.</note>, in octavo.

<summary>This page concludes a list of prominent Renaissance engineers and transitions into a formal bibliography of 17th and 18th-century scientific journals that documented "secret" philosophical sciences and natural magic.</summary>

<keywords>Georgius Agricola, Jacques Besson, Agostino Ramelli, Gaspar Schott, ventilators, scientific journals, Acta Eruditorum, Leopoldina, history of bibliography, natural magic</keywords>

--- Page 387 ---
<meta>This page continues the registry of scientific and philosophical periodicals, specifically focusing on journals and catalogs that document rare or "exotic" works in natural philosophy and magic.</meta>

<page-num>367</page-num>

*New Acts of the Academy of Lovers of the Exotic in Nature and Art* <note>original Latin: "Acta nova academiae philexoticorum naturae et artis." This was a short-lived journal edited by the Jesuit inventor Francesco Lana de Terzi.</note>. Leipzig, 1687. In octavo <note><term>octavo</term> — a book size formed by folding a printed sheet three times to create eight leaves.</note>. A work by the famous Father Lana, a Jesuit <note>Francesco Lana de Terzi (1631–1687) was an Italian Jesuit priest and mathematician who is often called the "father of aeronautics" for his early designs for vacuum-based flight.</note>.

*Journal of the Learned* <note>original French: "Journal des Sçavans." Established in 1665, it was the first academic journal published in Europe.</note>.
*Foreign Journal* <note>original French: "Journal étranger." A periodical dedicated to making foreign literature and science known in France.</note>.
*Journal of Trévoux* <note>original French: "Journal de Trevoux." A prominent Jesuit scholarly journal known for its critiques of Enlightenment philosophy.</note>.
*Catalog of the King’s Library* <note>original French: "Catalogue de la bibliotheque du Roi." The royal collection that eventually became the National Library of France.</note>.
*Journal of Paris* <note>original French: "Journal de Paris." The first daily French newspaper.</note>.
*Bünau Catalog* <note>original French: "Catalogue de Bünau." Refers to the massive private library of Count Heinrich von Bünau, which was one of the most comprehensive of the 18th century.</note>.
*Journal of Physics* <note>original French: "Journal de Physique."</note>.
*Catalog of the Curiosities of Nature* <note>original French: "Catalogue des curieux de la nature." References the publications or collections associated with the Leopoldina academy.</note>.
*Journal of Verdun* <note>original French: "Journal de Verdun." A monthly historical and literary periodical.</note>.
*Catalog of Count Firmian* <note>original French: "Catalogue du Comte de Firmian." Referring to the library of Carlo Gotthardo von Firmian, a notable governor of Lombardy and a bibliophile.</note>.
*Memoirs of the Academy of Sciences* <note>original French: "Memoires de l'Academie des sciences." The official record of the French Royal Academy.</note>.
*Selected Works of the Charitable and Scientific Society* <note>original Latin: "Analecta societatis charit. et scient."</note>.
*The Aprosian Library* <note>original Latin: "Bibliotheca Aprosiana." A famous catalog of the library of Angelico Aprosio, a 17th-century scholar.</note>.
— *The Crofts Library* <note>Refers to the auction catalog of the library of Thomas Crofts, a famous 18th-century English book collector.</note>.
*Encyclopedia* <note>Likely the "Encyclopédie" of Diderot and d’Alembert, the quintessential summary of Enlightenment knowledge.</note>.
*Mercury of France* <note>original French: "Mercure de France." A gazette covering court news, literature, and science.</note>.

For enthusiasts who would wish to establish for themselves a library of such books, or for those who have access to any distinguished libraries, there is appended a registry of the most notable authors and many rare and unknown works of this kind.

### A.

Baier and Buerius.

# Page 4

[This page consists of a full-page <term>marbled paper<gloss>A decorative paper created by floating pigments on a liquid base and transferring the pattern to paper, often used in bookbinding.</gloss></term> <term>endpaper<gloss>The leaf of paper at the very beginning or end of a book, used to help secure the book block to its cover.</gloss></term>]

->The design features a "stone" pattern in shades of blue, white, and grey, accented with fine dark veins.<-

<note>The "stone" pattern seen here was a popular style of marbling beginning in the 17th century. It was created by dropping paint onto a bath of gum water or carrageenan moss; the first colors dropped would shrink into small "veins" as subsequent colors were added, resulting in an appearance similar to polished marble or granite.</note>

<summary>A decorative endpaper featuring a blue, white, and grey marbled stone pattern, serving as a transitional element between the binding and the text.</summary>
<keywords>marbled paper, endpaper, bookbinding, stone pattern, 17th-century style, decorative arts</keywords>

# Front Cover

The manuscript is bound in a sturdy <term>leather binding<gloss>The protective outer covering of a volume, typically made of animal hide stretched over boards.</gloss></term>. The leather is a warm, mottled brown, though it shows extensive signs of age and use, including visible wear, scratches, and dark staining across the entire surface.

A simple <term>blind-tooled<gloss>A decorative technique where a design is pressed into the leather using a heated tool, leaving an impression without any gold leaf or color.</gloss></term> border follows the perimeter of the cover. This frame consists of:

*   A single straight line
*   A repeating wave or scallop pattern

<note>The lack of gold leaf (gilding) and the simplicity of the tooling suggests a utilitarian binding meant for protection and regular use rather than a high-status luxury display piece.</note>

<summary>The exterior of the manuscript is a worn, mottled brown leather binding featuring simple blind-tooled decorative borders along the edges.</summary>

# Back Cover

The back cover of the manuscript is bound in the same sturdy <term>leather binding<gloss>The protective outer covering of a volume, typically made of animal hide stretched over boards.</gloss></term> as the front. The leather is a warm, mottled brown, though it exhibits significant signs of age, characterized by scuffs and dark patches across the surface.

A <term>blind-tooled<gloss>A decorative technique where a design is pressed into the leather using a heated tool, leaving an impression without any gold leaf or color.</gloss></term> border is visible around the edges, mirroring the decorative pattern found on the front cover.

The <term>spine<gloss>The part of the binding where the pages are joined and which faces out when the book is on a shelf.</gloss></term> of the volume shows considerable wear. It appears cracked and brittle, with a visible loss of material at both the <term>head<gloss>The top edge of the book spine.</gloss></term> and the <term>tail<gloss>The bottom edge of the book spine.</gloss></term>.

<note>The physical condition of the spine, particularly the loss of leather at the top and bottom, suggests that the volume was frequently pulled from a shelf and accessed over several centuries.</note>

<summary>The back cover of the manuscript features a worn, mottled brown leather binding with blind-tooled borders and a heavily weathered spine showing material loss at the top and bottom.</summary>
